Relative algebroids and Cartan realization problems Thanks: This work was partially supported by NSF grant DMS-2303586 and UIUC Campus Research Board Award RB25014.
Rui Loja Fernandes, Wilmer Smilde

Abstract
. We introduce the notion of a relative algebroid to address existence and classification problems for geometric structures governed by partial differential equations. This framework unifies the theories of ordinary Lie algebroids and (formal) partial differential equations. We study prolongation and formal integrability of a relative algebroid, leading to a profinite Lie algebroid that governs the classification problem. At the core of this new framework is the notion of a relative derivation, whose theory we develop in detail. Throughout, we illustrate the theory with numerous examples.



Introduction

The role of algebroids in certain classification problems was first explicitly recognized by Bryant in his work on the classification of Bochner-Kähler metrics
[
7
]
. Struchiner and the first author built on this insight, establishing precise connections between existence and classification problems and the integrability of the underlying Lie algebroid
[
17
,
19
,
18
]
, particularly in relation with the
global
solutions to the classification problem. However, their work is restricted to cases where the local classification is finite-dimensional in nature, which excludes most examples. In this paper, we initiate the study of classification problems without this restriction. For this, we introduce the new concept of
relative Lie algebroid
, which provides a powerful tool for understanding general existence and classification problems for geometric structures. Moreover, the framework of relative algebroids, besides explaining known examples of algebroid-like structures, provides a systematic and coordinate-invariant language for understanding geometric structures defined by invariant PDEs.

The notion of a relative algebroid has roots in the work of Bryant, particularly in his unpublished notes on Lie Theory and Exterior Differential Systems
[
8
]
. There, he observed through various examples that many existence problems can be recast in a particular form —referred to here as
Bryant’s equations
— which resembles an algebroid but is not quite one
[
6
,
8
]
. Although Bryant did not identify this notion explicitly, he showed that techniques going back to Cartan can be applied to solve these equations
locally
in many interesting examples. Our ultimate aim is to understand the
global
(or complete, maximal) solutions to a classification problem. The framework of relative algebroids enables the use of integration techniques from Lie theory to solve global aspects of generic geometric problems.

The algebraic structure underlying the notion of a relative algebroid is what we call a
relative derivation
(also called an
augmented derivation
on
nLab
). A significant part of the paper is dedicated to developing the theory of such relative derivations in the context of differential geometry. To our knowledge, these objects have not yet been systematically studied in the literature, although they appear to be present in many other contexts, such as deformation theory and control theory.

In the rest of this introduction, we will first explain what Bryant’s equations are, where they come from and how they are used in existence and classification problems. Then, we will briefly describe the contents of this paper.

Bryant’s equations and two examples

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold. Recall that its orthogonal frame bundle

\pi\colon P\to M.

carries a coframe

(\theta,\omega)\colon TP\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{o}(n).

whose components are the tautological form (also called the solder form) and the Levi-Civita connection 1-form. Together they satisfy Cartan’s structure equations

(0.1)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}\theta=-\omega\wedge\theta,\\
{\mathrm{d}}\omega=R(\theta\wedge\theta)-\omega\wedge\omega,\end{cases}

where
R\colon P\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathfrak{o}(n))
is a map inducing the Riemann curvature tensor when passing to the associate bundles.

The structure equations (
0.1
) completely characterize the orthonormal frame bundles of Riemannian manifolds, and allow one to relate existence and classification problems for Riemannian structures to those of coframes, as observed by Cartan himself. In a classification problem one typically has restrictions on the curvature
R
in the form of an algebraic or a differential equation (or both). Let us recall one simple example where one already sees the appearance of a Lie algebroid.

Example 0.1

(Space forms)
.

For the classification of Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
with constant sectional curvature, a.k.a.
space forms
, the Riemann curvature
R\colon P\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathfrak{o}(n))
takes the special form:

R(K)(u,v)w=K\left(\langle w,u\rangle v-\langle w,v\rangle u\right),

where
K
is a constant (the scalar curvature),
u,v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. So one considers Cartan’s structure equations with this specific shape of the tensor together with the equation that
K
is constant:

(0.2)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}\theta=-\omega\wedge\theta\\
{\mathrm{d}}\omega=R(K)(\theta\wedge\theta)-\omega\wedge\omega\\
{\mathrm{d}}K=0.\end{cases}

These are Bryant’s equations for this classification problem.

Equations (
0.2
) already show the appearance of a Lie algebroid behind this classification problem of space forms. We “forget” about the underlying manifold these equations were derived from and look at the system “
as is
”. More precisely, we consider the trivial vector bundle
A=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{o}(n)}\to\mathbb{R}
, interpret
K
as a coordinate function on the base, and view
\theta
and
\omega
as generating sections of the vector bundle
A^{*}
. Equations (
0.2
) then define a linear operator

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(A),\quad\text{where }\Omega^{k}(A):=\Gamma(\wedge^{k}A^{*}).

This is a graded derivation satisfying
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
, hence it defines a Lie algebroid structure on
A
. In
[
19
]
it is explained how integration of this Lie algebroid leads to the well-known classification of space forms.

In the previous example the “moduli space” of solutions is one-dimensional: there is only one invariant, the scalar curvature. For such classification problems of finite type, a complete theory was developed in
[
17
,
19
,
18
]
. However, in practice, such problems are rarely encountered. Our next example illustrates a more typical occurrence.

Example 0.2

(Surfaces with
|\nabla K|=1
,
[
8
, §5.1]
)
.

Consider the problem of classifying Riemann surfaces
(M,g)
whose scalar curvature
K
satisfies the differential equation
|\nabla K|=1
.

Let
(P,\theta,\omega)
be the orthonormal frame bundle of such a surface and identify
\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{o}(2)\cong\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Writing
(\theta,\omega)=(\theta^{1},\theta^{2},\theta^{3})
. The structure equations become

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{1}=-\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}=\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{1},\\
{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{3}=K\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2},\end{cases}

where
K
is the Gauss curvature. Since
K
is an
O(2)
-invariant function on
P
, its derivative can be written in the form

{\mathrm{d}}K=K_{1}\theta^{1}+K_{2}\theta^{2}.

The equation
|\nabla K|=1
becomes
K_{1}^{2}+K_{2}^{2}=1
, so the components
K_{i}
are related through a single coordinate
\varphi
on the circle. The structure equations augmented by these conditions on the curvature take the form:

(0.3)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{1}=-\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}=\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{1},\\
{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{3}=K\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{d}}K=\cos(\varphi)\theta^{1}+\sin(\varphi)\theta^{2}.\end{cases}

These are Bryant’s equations for this classification problem.

As in the previous example, the next step is to “forget” about the manifold
P
and look at the equations “
as they are
”. So, as before, we consider the trivial vector bundle
A=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{R}
has coordinate
K
, and think of the
\theta^{i}
’s as generating sections of the dual vector bundle. But now we run into a problem: equations (
0.3
) define a degree 1 linear operator
{\mathrm{D}}
whose values depend on the extra variable
\varphi
. To solve this issue we consider the vector bundle
B=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
has coordinates
(\varphi,K)
, so that now

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(B)

If
p\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the projection, so that
B=p^{*}A
, it makes sense to call
{\mathrm{D}}
a
derivation relative to
p
. This does not define a Lie algebroid anymore since, for instance, the equation
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
doesn’t make sense at this point. So we cannot directly use integration techniques for Lie algebroids to obtain solutions.

One could try to look for a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{B}
on
B
that extends
{\mathrm{D}}
and squares to zero by adding the extra equation
{\mathrm{D}}_{B}^{2}K=0
. This implies

\displaystyle 0={\mathrm{D}}_{B}^{2}K

\displaystyle={\mathrm{D}}_{B}(\cos(\varphi)\theta^{1}+\sin(\varphi)\theta^{2})

\displaystyle=-\sin(\varphi){\mathrm{D}}_{B}\varphi\wedge\theta^{1}-\cos(\varphi)\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{2}+\cos(\varphi){\mathrm{D}}_{B}\varphi\wedge\theta^{2}+\sin(\varphi)\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{1}.

It follows that, for this equation to hold, we must have

(0.4)

{\mathrm{D}}_{B}\varphi=\theta^{3}+c_{0}(-\sin(\varphi)\theta^{1}+\cos(\varphi)\theta^{2}),

where
c_{0}
is a new independent variable. So, again
{\mathrm{D}}_{B}
is not an actual derivation but a derivation relative to the projection
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
,
(c_{0},\varphi,K)\mapsto(\varphi,K)
.

This process never stops, and for this reason there is no finite-dimensional Lie algebroid governing this classification problem. We will return to this example in Section
6
.

These, and many other examples, led Bryant to observe that many classification problems amount to solving a problem involving data consisting of a principal bundle
P
with a coframe
\theta\colon TP\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, together with functions
(a^{\mu},b^{\varrho})\colon P\to\mathbb{R}^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
, satisfying
Bryant’s equations
:

(0.5)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}c^{i}_{jk}(a)\,\theta^{j}\wedge\theta^{k},\\
{\mathrm{d}}a^{\mu}=F^{\mu}_{i}(a,b)\,\theta^{i},\end{cases}

for some given functions
c^{i}_{jk}\colon\mathbb{R}^{s}\to\mathbb{R}
and
F^{\mu}_{i}\colon\mathbb{R}^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}
.

As in the two problems above, “forgetting” about the underlying bundle
P
, treating
(a,b)
as independent coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\theta
as sections of the bundle dual to the trivial vector bundle
A:=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{s}
, Bryant’s equations define a degree 1, linear operator

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(B),\quad\text{with }B=p^{*}A,

where
p\colon\mathbb{R}^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}^{s}
is the projection.

PDEs and derivations

Degree 1 differential operators similar to the ones in the previous example are well-known in the formal theory of PDEs. For example, there one considers the so-called
total exterior differential
. In its simplest form, one has a function in jet space, say
f=f(x,y,u)
, where
x,y
are independent variables and
u
is a dependent variable, and then its total differential is given by

{\mathrm{D}}f:=({\mathrm{D}}_{x}f)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+({\mathrm{D}}_{y}f)\,{\mathrm{d}}y=(f_{x}+f_{u}u_{x}){\mathrm{d}}x+(f_{y}+f_{u}u_{y}){\mathrm{d}}y.

Similarly, for a 1-form
\alpha=a(x,y,u){\mathrm{d}}x+b(x,y,u){\mathrm{d}}y
its total differential is defined by

{\mathrm{D}}\alpha:=\big({\mathrm{D}}_{x}b-{\mathrm{D}}_{y}a\big){\mathrm{d}}x\wedge{\mathrm{d}}y=\big(b_{x}+b_{u}u_{x}-a_{y}-a_{u}u_{y}\big){\mathrm{d}}x\wedge{\mathrm{d}}y.

If
q\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the submersion
(x,y,u)\mapsto(x,y)
and we denote by
J^{1}q
its 1st jet bundle with coordinates
(x,y,u,u_{x},u_{y})
, then one can think of
{\mathrm{D}}
as a degree 1, linear operator

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(B),\quad\text{where }A:=q^{*}T\mathbb{R}^{2},\ B:=p^{*}A.

Relative derivations, relative algebroids and the contents of this paper

The common geometric structure underlying the previous examples is captured by a
relative derivation
. More precisely, given a vector bundle
A\to N
and a map
p\colon M\to N
, a degree 1 derivation relative to
p
is a linear operator

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(p^{*}A),

satisfying

{\mathrm{D}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=({\mathrm{D}}\alpha)\wedge p^{*}\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|}p^{*}\alpha\wedge{\mathrm{D}}\beta,

for homogeneous elements
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(A)
. The resemblance with Lie algebroids now becomes clear: a Lie algebroid is a degree 1 derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
on a vector bundle
A\to N
relative to the identity map, which additionally satisfies
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
. A general relative derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
cannot be squared since domain and codomain are distinct. Hence, the equation
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
does not seem to make sense. In this paper, we will develop the necessary theory to make sense of this in a natural way.

In Section
1
, we develop the theory of
relative degree
k
derivations
, for arbitrary
k
. Just like ordinary degree
k
derivations which can be viewed, dually, as
k
-nary brackets, relative derivations can be viewed, dually, as
relative
k
-brackets
. Besides analyzing the structure of relative multiderivations and relative multibrackets, we will extend several notions from the theory of (formal) PDEs to this setting. These include

•

a notion of a
tableau (bundle) of derivations
,

•

a
Spencer complex
for tableaux of derivations,

•

involutivity
and
Cartan characters
for relative derivations.

The computations and examples in
[
8
]
suggest that Bryant may have already been aware of such notions. We intend to place his computations within a natural and robust framework.

In Section
2
, we introduce and study the central concept in this paper, namely the notion of
relative algebroid
. This is a triple
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
, where
A\to N
is a vector bundle and
{\mathrm{D}}
is a degree 1 derivation relative to a map
p\colon M\to N
. As we already pointed out, these are the geometric objects underlying Bryant’s equations. Similar to the theory developed in
[
7
,
17
,
19
]
, solutions to Bryant’s equations are translated to the notion of
realization
of a relative algebroid, so one speaks of
Cartan’s realization problem
. In the language of derivations, a realization allows to realize a relative derivation as a manifold with the de Rham differential. More precisely, given a relative algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
, a realization is a manifold
P
together with a bundle map
\theta\colon TP\to p^{*}A
that is fiberwise an isomorphism and is also a morphism of relative algebroids, i.e., satisfies

{\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}\circ p^{*}=\theta^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}.

In the case of finite-type problems, realizations naturally appear as fibers of an integrating Lie groupoid, making it possible to apply the power of Lie theory to solve classification problems (see
[
17
,
19
]
). In the general case, however, the situation is much more complex. As we will see, the theory of relative derivations unifies Lie algebroids and PDEs.

We will show first that the formal theory of the existence of realizations parallels that of partial differential equations, as developed by Spencer and his school, and later formalized by Goldschmidt in
[
20
]
. Specifically, we will show that first-order and second-order obstructions to the existence of realizations are captured by Spencer cohomology classes, called the
torsion class
and the
curvature class
.

In Section
3
, we will see that the vanishing of the torsion class leads to the notion of prolongation, which seeks to complete the derivation of a relative algebroid to an operator that squares to zero. More concretely, a
prolongation
of a relative algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
is another relative algebroid
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
, with projection
p_{1}\colon M^{(1)}\to M
. Here,
B=p^{*}A
and the derivation is a map
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(B)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(B^{(1)})
, where
B^{(1)}:=p_{1}^{*}B
, such that
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\circ{\mathrm{D}}=0
. Schematically, this is described by the diagram

\textstyle{B^{(1)}=p_{1}^{*}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B=p^{*}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}}
\textstyle{M^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{N}

The existence of the prolongation is contingent on the vanishing of the torsion class. Higher prolongations are obtained by iterating the first prolongations and are indexed by
k
. A relative algebroid is called
k
-integrable
if all prolongations up to order
k
exist, and is called
formally integrable
if it is
k
-integrable for all
k\geq 1
. Foundational results from the theory of formal PDEs, such as Goldschmidt’s formal integrability theorem
[
20
, Thm. 8.1]
, have natural generalizations to the theory of relative algebroids, namely we will prove the following analogue (or rather, extension) of that result for relative algebroids.

Theorem 1

(Theorem
3.10
)
.

Let
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid with tableau of derivations
\tau
. Suppose that:

(i)

(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
is 1-integrable;

(ii)

the Spencer cohomology groups
H^{k,2}(\tau)
vanish for all
k\geq 0
.

Then
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
is formally integrable.

If, for some
k
, the map
p_{k}\colon M^{(k)}\to M^{(k-1)}
is a diffeomorphism, the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}^{(k)}
actually defines the structure of a Lie algebroid, and we are in the
finite-type
case. However, in general – as in Example
0.2
– this process does not stop, and the full prolongation tower defines a
profinite Lie algebroid.
For this reason, we call a relative algebroid a
relative
Lie
algebroid
if all prolongations exist. Finding solutions to the realization problem in this more general case is much more challenging. The only general statement we can make is in the
analytic
setting, where an existence result, essentially due to Cartan and Kähler – see Bryant
[
8
, Thm. 3 and Thm. 4]
– holds. We state it as follows:

Theorem 2

(Theorem
3.20
)
.

Let
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
be an analytic relative Lie algebroid. For each
k
and each
x\in M^{(k)}
, there exists a realization through
x
.

In Section
4
, we consider several important constructions with relative algebroids. First, given some vector bundle
A\to N
, we construct a
universal relative algebroid

(A,p_{1},\breve{\mathrm{D}})
, which is formally integrable, with the property that every algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
relative to a submersion
p\colon M\to N
is a pullback of the universal one via a classifying map. Then we discuss the operation of
restriction
to subspaces and how this operation interacts with prolongations and realizations. Finally, we introduce the notion of
systatic foliation
of a relative algebroid, which captures the directions in which the tableau map is zero. We show that these directions are essentially “redundant” from the perspective of the realization problem, since the original almost relative algebroid descends to a reduced almost relative algebroid, which has essentially the same realizations and the same integrability properties.

In Section
5
, our discussion comes to a full circle by showing that any partial differential equation can be recast as a relative algebroid in such a way that the formal theory of prolongations of PDEs coincides with the prolongation theory for relative algebroids. For this, we first interpret the Cartan distribution on a jet space as a relative connection, and then we show that any relative connection has an associated relative algebroid. The derivation corresponding to the Cartan distribution is nothing more than the horizontal differential in the first row of the variational bicomplex. Then, given a
k
-th order PDE
E
viewed as a submanifold in the jet space
J^{k}q
of a submersion
q\colon N\to X
, by pulling back the Cartan derivation one obtains the associated relative algebroid. We will prove the following result:

Theorem 3

(Theorem
5.12
)
.

Let
E\subset J^{k}q
be a PDE. Then, germs of solutions to
E
are in one-to-one correspondence with germs of realizations of the associated relative algebroid, modulo diffeomorphisms. Moreover:

(i)

E
is a 1-integrable PDE if and only if the associated relative algebroid is 1-integrable.

(ii)

If
E
is a 1-integrable PDE, then the relative algebroid corresponding to its first prolongation
E^{(1)}\subset J^{k+1}q
is the prolongation of the relative algebroid associated with
E
.

In particular, a PDE is formally integrable if and only if its associated relative algebroid is.

In Section
6
, we return to Example
0.2
and discuss its solutions (i.e., realizations) in light of the theory developed in the previous sections.

The reader will notice that this paper lays only the foundations of the theory. There are many promising directions to explore, often involving connections to existing literature and related fields of mathematics. We conclude the paper with an outlook on future work in Section
7
.

1.
Relative derivations

In this section, we develop the theory of relative derivations. While after this section, we will only encounter relative derivations of degree 1 and 2, we consider here relative derivations of arbitrary degree. We believe that higher-degree relative derivations, like their ordinary counterparts, will prove useful in other applications.

1.1.
Derivations and brackets

We recall here some basic facts about
k
-derivations and
k
-brackets. For more details and proofs we refer the reader to
[
15
]
.

Let
V\to N
be a vector bundle and set
\Omega^{\bullet}(V):=\Gamma(\wedge^{\bullet}V^{*})
. A
k
-derivation
on
V
is a graded derivation of
\Omega^{\bullet}(V)
of degree
k
, i.e., a linear map
{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(V)\to\Omega^{\bullet+k}(V)
satisfying

{\mathrm{D}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=\left({\mathrm{D}}\alpha\right)\wedge\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|k}\alpha\wedge\left({\mathrm{D}}\beta\right).

for homogeneous
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(V)
.
The
symbol
of a
k
-derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
is the bundle map
\sigma({\mathrm{D}})\colon T^{*}N\to\wedge^{k}V^{*}
defined by

\sigma({\mathrm{D}})({\mathrm{d}}f)={\mathrm{D}}(f),\mbox{ for $f\in C^{\infty}(N)$}.

Any derivation is determined by its symbol and its action on a generating set (over
C^{\infty}(N)
) of
\Omega^{1}(V)
.

A dual point of view to multi-derivations is via multi-brackets. A
k
-bracket on a vector bundle
V\to N
is a skew-symmetric,
\mathbb{R}
-multilinear, map

[\cdot,\dots,\cdot]\colon\underbrace{\Gamma(V)\times\cdots\times\Gamma(V)}_{\text{$(k+1)$-times}}\to\Gamma(V)

together with an anchor
\rho\colon\wedge^{k}V\to TN
satisfying the Leibniz rule:

[v_{0},\cdots,fv_{k}]=f[v_{0},\cdots,v_{k}]+\rho(v_{0},\dots,v_{k-1})(f)v_{k},

for
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}\in\Gamma(V),f\in C^{\infty}(N)
.

The notions of
k
-brackets and
k
-derivations are in duality through the Koszul formula (see
[
15
, §2.5]
):

\displaystyle\langle\sigma({\mathrm{D}})({\mathrm{d}}f),v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathrm{d}}f,\rho(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}\alpha(v_{0},\dots,v_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\rho(v_{0},\dots,\widehat{v_{i}},\dots,v_{k})(\alpha(v_{i}))-\alpha([v_{0},\dots,v_{k}]),

for
f\in C^{\infty}(N)
,
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(V)
and
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}\in\Gamma(V)
.

Proposition 1.1
.

The space of
k
-derivations on
V
, denoted
\operatorname{Der}^{k}(V)
, is the space of sections of a vector bundle
\mathcal{D}_{V}^{k}\to N
, and the symbol induces a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(V^{*},\wedge^{k+1}V^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{V}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(T^{*}N,\wedge^{k}V^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Dually, using the canonical identification
\operatorname{Hom}(W^{*},V^{*})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(V,W)
, one has

(1.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{V}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}V,TN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

which is the sequence that we will use in practice.

A choice of connection
\nabla
on
V
determines a splitting
\mathcal{D}_{V}^{k}\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}V,V)
of this sequence. Namely, for any
k
-bracket, the expression

(1.2)

[v_{0},\dots,v_{k}]_{\nabla}:=[v_{0},\dots,v_{k}]+(-1)^{k+1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\nabla_{\rho(v_{0},\dots,\widehat{v_{i}},\dots,v_{k})}v_{i},

is
C^{\infty}(M)
-multilinear.

Example 1.2
.

A derivation
{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(V)\to\Omega^{\bullet}(V)
of degree 0 corresponds to a linear vector field on
V
. The flow
\varphi_{t}
of this linear vector field is a 1-parameter family of vector bundle maps that solves the ODE

\begin{cases}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\varphi_{t}^{*}\alpha=\varphi^{*}_{t}({\mathrm{D}}\alpha),\\
\varphi_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{V}.\end{cases}

Example 1.3
.

A Lie algebroid structure on a vector bundle
A\to M
is 1-derivation
{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(A)
such that
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
.

1.2.
Relative derivations

Fix two vector bundles
W\to M
and
V\to N
together with bundle map
(\varphi,p)\colon W\to V
covering a smooth map
p\colon M\to N
.

Definition 1.4
.

A
k
-derivation relative to
\varphi
is a map

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(V)\to\Omega^{\bullet+k}(W)

satisfying the Leibniz rule

{\mathrm{D}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=({\mathrm{D}}\alpha)\wedge(\varphi^{*}\beta)+(-1)^{|\alpha|k}(\varphi^{*}\alpha)\wedge({\mathrm{D}}\beta).

The space of
k
-derivations relative to
\varphi
is denoted by
\operatorname{Der}^{k}(\varphi)
.

Lemma 1.5
.

The space
\operatorname{Der}^{k}(\varphi)
arises as the space of sections of a vector bundle
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
over
M
.

Proof.

The space
\operatorname{Der}^{k}(\varphi)
is a
C^{\infty}(M)
-module. In local trivializations and coordinates, it is clear that this module is also locally finitely generated. The lemma follows from the Serre-Swan theorem.
∎

Note that ordinary derivations are derivations relative to the identity map, so that
\operatorname{Der}^{k}(V)=\operatorname{Der}^{k}(\operatorname{id}_{V})
and
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{V}=\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\operatorname{id}_{V}}
.

Definition 1.6
.

The
symbol
of a
k
-derivation relative to
(\varphi,p)
is the map
\sigma({\mathrm{D}})\colon p^{*}T^{*}N\to\wedge^{k}W^{*}
defined by

\sigma({\mathrm{D}})_{x}({\mathrm{d}}_{p(x)}f)={\mathrm{D}}(f)_{x}\in\wedge^{k}W^{*}|_{x},\quad\text{ for }f\in C^{\infty}(N),\ x\in M.

The dual of the symbol, arising from the canonical identification
\operatorname{Hom}(W,V)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(V^{*},W^{*})
, is also denoted by
\sigma({\mathrm{D}})\colon\wedge^{k}W\to p^{*}TN
. As for ordinary derivations, the symbol induces an exact sequence

(1.3)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,p^{*}V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,p^{*}TN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Example 1.7
.

If
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\in\operatorname{Der}^{k}(W)
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{2}\in\operatorname{Der}^{k}(V)
are
k
-derivations on
W
and
V
, respectively, then
\varphi^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ\varphi^{*}
are both derivations relative to
\varphi
.

Example 1.8
.

Let
V\to N
be a vector bundle and fix
x\in N
. Any element
{\mathrm{D}}\in(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{V})_{x}
determines a
k
-derivation
relative
to the inclusion
\iota_{x}\colon V_{x}\hookrightarrow V
:

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(V)\to\wedge^{\bullet+k}V_{x}^{*}

It can be defined as follows. Pick any section
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{V})
with
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{x}={\mathrm{D}}
and set

{\mathrm{D}}\alpha=\left(\iota_{x}^{*}\circ\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\right)\alpha.

This gives a canonical identification between the space of
k
-derivations relative to the inclusion
\iota_{x}\colon V_{x}\hookrightarrow V
and the fiber
(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{V})_{x}
.

The most important case relevant to Bryant’s equations occurs when
V
is a vector bundle over
N
,
W=p^{*}V
is the pullback of
V
along a map
p\colon M\to N
and
\varphi=p_{*}\colon p^{*}V\to V
is the canonical projection – see also Remark
1.11
below. In this case, the short exact sequence (
1.3
) becomes

(1.4)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{p^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}V,TN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The previous sequence suggests the following result.

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
p\colon M\to N
be a map,
V\to N
a vector bundle and
p_{*}\colon p^{*}V\to V
the projection. Then there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{D}^{k}_{p_{*}}\cong p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{k}_{V}.

Proof.

This follows from the observation that

\operatorname{Der}^{k}(p_{*})\cong C^{\infty}(M)\otimes_{C^{\infty}(N)}\operatorname{Der}^{k}(V)

as a
C^{\infty}(M)
-module.
∎

1.3.
Relative brackets

Relative derivations are in duality with relative brackets, a concept that we will introduce here.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
V\to N
be a vector bundle and
p\colon M\to N
any smooth map. A
k
-bracket relative to
p
is a skew-symmetric
\mathbb{R}
-multilinear map

[\cdot,\dots,\cdot]\colon\underbrace{\Gamma(V)\times\cdots\times\Gamma(V)}_{\text{$(k+1)$-times}}\to\Gamma(p^{*}V)

and a relative anchor
\rho\colon\wedge^{k}p^{*}V\to p^{*}TN
satisfying the Leibniz rule

[v_{0},\dots,fv_{k}]=(p^{*}f)[v_{0},\dots,v_{k}]+\rho(p^{*}v_{0},\dots,p^{*}v_{k-1})(f)p^{*}v_{k}

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(N)
.

Remark 1.11
.

There seems to be no canonical way to define a bracket relative to an arbitrary bundle map
(\varphi,p)\colon W\to V
because there is no canonical map
\Gamma(V)\to\Gamma(W)
, unless
\varphi
is a fiberwise isomorphism, in which case
W
is isomorphic to
p^{*}V
.

Similar to the case of ordinary multiderivations, we have the following correspondence between relative multiderivations and relative multibrackets.

Lemma 1.12
.

Let
p\colon M\to N
be a map and
V\to N
a vector bundle. There is a 1:1 correspondence between

(i)

k
-derivations relative to
p_{*}\colon p^{*}V\to V
and

(ii)

k
-brackets relative to
p
.

Proof.

This follows from a modified version of the Koszul formula:

\displaystyle({\mathrm{D}}f)(p^{*}v_{1},\dots,p^{*}v_{k})

\displaystyle=\rho(p^{*}v_{1},\dots,p^{*}v_{k})(f),

\displaystyle({\mathrm{D}}\alpha)(p^{*}v_{0},\dots,p^{*}v_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i+k}\rho(p^{*}v_{0},\dots,\widehat{p^{*}v_{i}},\dots,p^{*}v_{k})\left(\langle\alpha,v_{i}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-p^{*}\alpha([v_{0},\dots,v_{k}])

for
f\in C^{\infty}(N)
,
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(V)
and
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}\in\Gamma(V)
.
∎

Remark 1.13
.

Thinking of multiderivations relative to
p
as multibrackets relative to
p
, one sees that each choice of connection
\nabla
for
V
gives a splitting of the sequence (
1.4
) associated with the anchor. Namely, we have the analogue of formula (
1.2
): given a relative
k
-bracket the expression

[p^{*}v_{0},\dots,p^{*}v_{k}]_{\nabla}:=[v_{0},\dots,v_{k}]+(-1)^{k+1}p^{*}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\nabla_{\rho(p^{*}v_{0},\dots,\widehat{p^{*}v_{i}},\dots,p^{*}v_{k})}v_{i}\Big),

defines a unique element of
\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}p^{*}V,p^{*}V)=p^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}V,V)
.

1.4.
Brackets and derivations relative to foliations

In practice, relative derivations often appear relative to a submersion
p\colon M\to N
. In some cases, however, we encounter derivations that are only
locally
relative to a submersion, i.e., they are relative to a foliation
\mathcal{F}
of
M
. In order to define them properly, we need to recall first some basic notions from foliation theory (see, e.g.,
[
25
]
).

1.4.1.
Foliated flat vector bundles

Henceforth, we will identify a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on a manifold
M
with an involutive subbundle
\mathcal{F}\subset TM
. Associated to
\mathcal{F}
one has the sheaf of
basic functions
given by

C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}(U)=\{f\in C^{\infty}(U):X(f)=0\text{ for all $X\in\Gamma(\mathcal{F}|_{U})$}\}.

Given a vector bundle
W\to M
, we will write
\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{F};W)
for the foliated
k
-forms with values in
W
, i.e., sections of
\wedge^{k}\mathcal{F}^{*}\otimes W
. Recall that a
\mathcal{F}
-connection
on
W
is a
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear map
\overline{\nabla}\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{F})\times\Gamma(W)\to\Gamma(W)
satisfying:

\overline{\nabla}_{fX}w=f\overline{\nabla}_{X},\quad\overline{\nabla}_{X}(fw)=f\overline{\nabla}_{x}w+X(f)w,

for
X\in\Gamma(\mathcal{F})
,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
w\in\Gamma(W)
. It can be interpreted as a differential operator
\overline{\nabla}\colon\Gamma(W)\to\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{F};W)
. A section
w\in\Gamma(W)
is
\overline{\nabla}
-parallel
when
\overline{\nabla}w=0
. This gives rise to a sheaf of modules over
C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
given by

\Gamma_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}(U)=\{w\in\Gamma_{W}(U):\overline{\nabla}w=0\}.

Local existence of parallel sections is controlled by the curvature of
{\overline{\nabla}}
which is the 2-form

R_{\overline{\nabla}}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{F};\operatorname{End}(W))
given by

R_{\overline{\nabla}}(X,Y)=\left[\overline{\nabla}_{X},\overline{\nabla}_{Y}\right]-\overline{\nabla}_{[X,Y]}.

For a flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection (i.e.
R_{\overline{\nabla}}=0
), there exist a parallel local section through every point
w\in W
. However, non-zero global parallel sections may not exist.

Definition 1.14
.

A
foliated vector bundle

(W,\overline{\nabla})
is a vector bundle
W
over a manifold with foliation
(M,\mathcal{F})
together with an
\mathcal{F}
-connection
\overline{\nabla}
. The foliated vector bundle is called
flat
when the
\mathcal{F}
-connection is flat.

The dual
W^{*}
of a foliated vector vector bundle
(W,\overline{\nabla})
carries a canonical
\mathcal{F}
-connection
\overline{\nabla}
determined by

\mathcal{L}_{X}\langle\alpha,w\rangle=\left\langle\overline{\nabla}_{X}\alpha,w\right\rangle+\left\langle\alpha,\overline{\nabla}_{X}w\right\rangle,

for
X\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}
,
\alpha\in\Gamma_{W^{*}}
and
w\in\Gamma_{W}
. Note that
(W^{*},\overline{\nabla})
is flat iff
(W,\overline{\nabla})
is flat.

Next, by a
map of foliations

p\colon(M_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to(M_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})
we mean a map such that
{\mathrm{d}}p(\mathcal{F}_{1})\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}
. For example, the identity
\operatorname{id}\colon M\to M
maps the trivial foliation to any foliation
\mathcal{F}_{M}
. A
map of foliated vector bundles

\varphi\colon(W_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1})\to(W_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2})
covering a map of foliations
p\colon(M_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to(M_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})
is a bundle map
\varphi\colon W_{1}\to W_{2}
which satisfies

{\overline{\nabla}}^{1}_{X}(\varphi^{*}\alpha))=\varphi^{*}({\overline{\nabla}}^{2}_{p_{*}(X)}\alpha),\quad\text{for all }X\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}_{1}},\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(W_{2}).

In the case of flat foliated bundles, this condition ensures that pullback maps flat forms to flat forms
\varphi^{*}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2})}\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1})}
.

Remark 1.15
.

When
\varphi
is a fiberwise isomorphism, we obtain also a pullback of sections which preserves flatness:

\varphi^{*}\colon\Gamma_{(W_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2})}\to\Gamma_{(W_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1})},\quad(\varphi^{*}w)(x):=\varphi^{-1}(w(p(x))).

There is a version of the Serre-Swan theorem for flat foliated vector bundles.

Proposition 1.16
.

Let
(M,\mathcal{F})
be a manifold with foliation. There is, up to a natural isomorphism, a one-to-one correspondence

\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{
locally finitely generated}\\
\\
\mbox{and locally free $C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{bas}}$-modules
}\\
\end{subarray}\right\}\overset{1:1}{\longleftrightarrow}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{flat foliated vector}\\
\\
\mbox{bundles over $(M,\mathcal{F})$}\\
\end{subarray}\right\}

Proof.

Given
(W,\overline{\nabla})\to M
, we just saw how to construct a locally finitely generated and locally free
C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
-module
\Gamma_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
. For the converse, given such a module
\Gamma_{\parallel}
, set

\Gamma_{W}:=\Gamma_{\parallel}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}}C^{\infty}

which is a locally finitely generated, locally free
C^{\infty}
-module. By the Serre-Swan theorem,
\Gamma_{W}
is the sheaf of sections of a vector bundle
W\to M
. The flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection is determined by requiring its local flat sections to satisfy

\overline{\nabla}_{X}w=0,\quad\text{ for all $X\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}$, $w\in\Gamma_{\parallel}$}.

It is well-defined because
\Gamma_{\parallel}
is a
C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
-module. These constructions are inverses of each other. Naturality follows from naturality of the original Serre-Swan theorem, observing that the resulting maps preserve the parallel sections, and so are maps of flat foliated vector bundles.
∎

Let
(W,\overline{\nabla})
be flat foliated vector bundle over a foliated manifold
(M,\mathcal{F})
. The
holonomy
representation of a leaf
L
of
\mathcal{F}
is denoted

h\colon\pi_{1}(L,x)\to\GL(W_{x})

and is obtained, as usual, by parallel transport along loops based at
x
. More generally, parallel transport along paths, gives the groupoid representation

\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F})\to\GL(W),

where
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F})\rightrightarrows M
is the Lie groupoid whose arrows are the leafwise homotopy classes of paths in
\mathcal{F}
, and
\GL(W)\rightrightarrows M
is the Lie groupoid whose arrows are the linear isomorphisms between the fibers of
W
. Conversely, any such Lie groupoid representation defines a flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection on
W
.

If
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}\subset\mathcal{F}
is a subfoliation, a flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection determines by restriction a flat
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
-connection and the holonomy representations are related by

\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{h}}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\GL(W)}

Proposition 1.17
.

Let
(M,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated manifold and
p\colon M\to N
a surjective submersion with connected fibers such that
\ker({\mathrm{d}}p)\subset\mathcal{F}
. Then there is a foliation
\mathcal{F}_{N}
on
N
such that
\mathcal{F}=({\mathrm{d}}p)^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{N})
and
p\colon(M,\mathcal{F})\to(N,\mathcal{F}_{N})
is a map of foliated manifolds, for which there is, up to a natural isomorphism, a one-to-one correspondence between

\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{vector bundles over $N$ with}\\
\\
\mbox{flat $\mathcal{F}_{N}$-connection}\end{subarray}\right\}\xleftrightarrow{1:1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{vector bundles over $M$ with}\\
\\
\mbox{flat $\mathcal{F}$-connection having}\\
\\
\mbox{trivial holonomy along $\ker({\mathrm{d}}p)$}\end{subarray}\right\}

Proof.

Since
p
is a surjective submersion and
\ker({\mathrm{d}}p)\subset\mathcal{F}
, it follows that

\mathcal{F}_{N}:={\mathrm{d}}p(\mathcal{F})

is a well-defined involutive subbundle of
TN
. Since the fibers of
p
are connected and contained in
\mathcal{F}
, one has that
\mathcal{F}=({\mathrm{d}}p)^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{N})
.

One direction of the correspondence is clear: since
p
is a map of foliations, if
(W_{N},\overline{\nabla}_{N})\to(N,\mathcal{F}_{N})
is a flat foliated vector bundle, then
(p^{*}W_{N},p^{*}\overline{\nabla}_{N})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
is a vector bundle with flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection with trivial holonomy along
\ker({\mathrm{d}}p)
.

For the converse, suppose we are given a vector bundle with a flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection
(W,\overline{\nabla})\to M
having trivial holonomy along
\mathcal{F}_{p}:=\ker({\mathrm{d}}p)
. Then the holonomy representation of
\mathcal{F}_{p}
factors via the submersion groupoid

\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\times_{p}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\GL(W)}

The resulting linear action of
M\times_{p}M\rightrightarrows M
on
W\to M
is free and proper. The quotient is a vector bundle
W_{N}\to N
and there is a canonical isomorphism

p^{*}W_{N}\cong W.

Moreover, the submersion
p\colon M\to N
induces a surjective groupoid morphism

p_{*}\colon\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F})\to\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{N})

whose kernel contains
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{p})
. It follows that the holonomy representation descends to a representation of
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{N})
making the following diagram commute.

\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\GL(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{N})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{N}}
\textstyle{\GL(W_{N})}

Hence, there is a unique flat
\mathcal{F}_{N}
-connection
{\overline{\nabla}}_{N}
on
W_{N}
whose holonomy representation is
h_{N}
. For such a connection one has

p^{*}{\overline{\nabla}}_{N}={\overline{\nabla}}.

The previous two constructions are inverse to each other so the result follows.
∎

Example 1.18
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be any foliation on
M
and denote by
\nu(\mathcal{F})=TM/\mathcal{F}
the normal bundle of
\mathcal{F}
. This carries a flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection, namely the
Bott connection

\overline{\nabla}^{\text{Bott}}_{X}\overline{Y}:=\overline{[X,Y]},\quad\mbox{ for $X\in\Gamma(\mathcal{F})$, $\overline{Y}\in\nu(\mathcal{F})$}.

When
\mathcal{F}
is a simple foliation, so that
N=M/\mathcal{F}
is a manifold, the resulting foliation on
N
is the trivial one:
\mathcal{F}_{N}=0_{N}
. The corresponding vector bundle over
N
is the tangent bundle
TN
(a flat foliated vector bundle for the trivial foliation).

More generally, any vector bundle
V\to N
is a flat foliated vector bundle for the trivial foliation, so the pullback
W:=p^{*}V
carries a canonical flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection
{\overline{\nabla}}
. It is the connection whose local flat sections are the sections of the form
p^{*}v
, with
v
any local section of
V
.

1.4.2.
Derivations relative to a foliation

Throughout the rest of this section, we fix a flat foliated vector bundle
(W,{\overline{\nabla}})
over
(M,\mathcal{F})
. The sheaf of sections of
\wedge^{\bullet}W^{*}\to M
(the “
W
-forms”) will be denoted by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{W}
, whereas the sheaf
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
of
{\overline{\nabla}}
-parallel
W
-forms is given by

\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}(U):=\{\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{W}(U):\overline{\nabla}\alpha=0\}.

Definition 1.19
.

A
parallel
k
-derivation
on a flat foliated vector bundle
(W,\overline{\nabla})
is a map of sheaves

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}\to\Omega^{\bullet+k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}

satisfying, for any homogeneous
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
, the Leibniz rule

{\mathrm{D}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=({\mathrm{D}}\alpha)\wedge\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|k}\alpha\wedge({\mathrm{D}}\beta).

The sheaf of parallel
k
-derivations is denoted by
\operatorname{Der}^{k}_{\parallel}(W,\overline{\nabla})
.

Lemma 1.20
.

\operatorname{Der}^{k}_{\parallel}(W,\overline{\nabla})
is the sheaf of flat sections of a flat foliated vector bundle, denoted
(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})},\overline{\nabla})
.

Proof.

\operatorname{Der}^{k}_{\parallel}(W,\overline{\nabla})
is a locally finitely generated, locally free
C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
-module. Thus, by Proposition
1.16
, it is the space of parallel sections of a flat foliated vector bundle
(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})},\overline{\nabla})
.
∎

We are now ready to introduce the following generalization of derivations relative to submersions to derivations relative to foliations.

Definition 1.21
.

A
k
-derivation on
(W,\overline{\nabla})
relative to
\mathcal{F}
is a map of sheaves

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}\to\Omega^{\bullet+k}_{W},

satisfying for homogeneous
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
the Leibniz rule

{\mathrm{D}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=({\mathrm{D}}\alpha)\wedge\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|k}\alpha\wedge({\mathrm{D}}\beta).

Its
symbol
is the map
\sigma({\mathrm{D}})\in\operatorname{Hom}(\nu^{*}(\mathcal{F}),\wedge^{k}W^{*})
given by

\sigma({\mathrm{D}})({\mathrm{d}}f)={\mathrm{D}}(f),\quad\text{for $f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}$.}

In practice, we will think of the symbol as a map
\sigma({\mathrm{D}})\colon\wedge^{k}W\to\nu(\mathcal{F})
via the usual canonical identification.

Remark 1.22
.

The relative
k
-derivations just defined are canonically identified with the global sections of the vector bundle
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
, while parallel
k
-derivations correspond to parallel sections of
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}})}
. The flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection on sections of
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}})}
is given by

({\overline{\nabla}}_{X}{\mathrm{D}})\alpha={\overline{\nabla}}_{X}({\mathrm{D}}\alpha),\quad\mbox{ for $\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}})}$}.

Unlike global parallel
k
-derivations, which may or may not exist, relative
k
-derivations always exist in abundance.

The duality between brackets and derivations relative to submersions extends to foliations.

Definition 1.23
.

A
k
-bracket on
(W,\overline{\nabla})
relative to
\mathcal{F}
is a skew-symmetric
\mathbb{R}
-multilinear map of sheaves

[\cdot,\dots,\cdot]\colon\underbrace{\Gamma_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}}_{\text{$(k+1)$-times}}\to\Gamma_{W}

with a relative anchor
\rho\colon\wedge^{k}W\to\nu(\mathcal{F})
satisfying the Leibniz rule

[w_{0},\dots,fw_{k}]=f[w_{0},\dots,w_{k}]+\rho(w_{0},\dots,w_{k-1})(f)w_{k}

for any local flat sections
w_{i}
and basic function

f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
.

In a manner entirely similar to Lemma
1.12
, we find:

Lemma 1.24
.

Let
(W,{\overline{\nabla}})
be a flat foliated vector bundle over
(M,\mathcal{F})
. There is a 1:1 correspondence between

(i)

k
-derivations relative to
\mathcal{F}
and

(ii)

k
-brackets relative to
\mathcal{F}
.

Notice that in the case of a submersion
p\colon M\to N
and a vector bundle
V\to N
– see Example
1.18
– these definitions and results specialize to the previous notions of
k
-derivation and
k
-bracket relative to
p\colon M\to N
.

1.4.3.
The structure of relative derivations

We start by giving the analogue of the short exact sequence (
1.4
). The proof is immediate.

Lemma 1.25
.

Given a foliated flat bundle
(W,{\overline{\nabla}})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
, the symbol map induces a short exact sequence

(1.5)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,\nu(\mathcal{F}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

If we equip these bundles with the induced connections
{\overline{\nabla}}
and
\overline{\nabla}\otimes\overline{\nabla}^{\mathrm{Bott}}
(for the last term), this is a sequence of flat foliated vector bundles.

An
extension
of the
\mathcal{F}
-connection
\overline{\nabla}
to an ordinary connection
\nabla
is a connection on
W
such that

\nabla_{X}w=\overline{\nabla}_{X}w,\quad\text{for all }X\in\Gamma(\mathcal{F}),\ w\in\Gamma(W).

It induces a well-defined map

\nabla\colon\Gamma_{\nu(\mathcal{F})}\times\Gamma_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}\to\Gamma_{W},\quad\nabla_{[X]}w:=\nabla_{X}w.

The extension
\nabla
is called
\mathcal{F}
-parallel
when this map takes values in
\Gamma_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
. While extensions always exist, parallel extensions are only guaranteed to exist locally.

Extensions of
\overline{\nabla}
yield splittings of the previous short exact sequence.

Lemma 1.26
.

Let
(W,{\overline{\nabla}})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
be flat foliated bundle. An extension
\nabla
of
\overline{\nabla}
induces a splitting of the short exact sequence (
1.5
) so that

\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,W)\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,\nu(\mathcal{F})).

If the extension is
\mathcal{F}
-parallel, then this splitting is an isomorphism of flat foliated vector bundles.

Proof.

For any extension
\nabla
we can define a splitting
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,W)
of (
1.5
) similar to Remark
1.13
: given a relative bracket in
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
the expression

[w_{0},\dots,w_{k}]_{\nabla}:=[w_{0},\dots,w_{k}]+(-1)^{k+1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\nabla_{\rho(w_{0},\dots,\widehat{w_{i}},\dots,w_{k})}w_{i},

which is defined for local flat sections
w_{i}\in\Gamma_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}})}
, extends to a unique
C^{\infty}
-linear map, determining a bundle map
\wedge^{k+1}W\to W
. When
\nabla
is an
\mathcal{F}
-parallel extension, this bundle map sends
\mathcal{F}
-flat sections to
\mathcal{F}
-flat sections, so it is map of foliated flat bundles.
∎

Any
k
-derivation
{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{W})
can be restricted to
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
to yield a derivation
\Pi({\mathrm{D}})
relative to
\mathcal{F}
. This restriction map is
C^{\infty}
-linear, so it is induced from a bundle map
\Pi\colon\mathcal{D}^{k}_{W}\to\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
.

Lemma 1.27
.

There is a short exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise where the inclusion
\iota
is defined at the level of sections by

\iota(\rho)(\alpha)(w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l})=\frac{1}{k!l!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k+l}}(-1)^{\sigma}\left(\overline{\nabla}_{\rho(w_{\sigma(1)},\dots,w_{\sigma(k)})}\alpha\right)(w_{\sigma(k+1)},\dots,w_{\sigma(k+l)}),

for
\alpha\in\Omega^{l}(W)
and
w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l}\in\Gamma(W)
.

Remark 1.28
.

The map
\iota\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,\mathcal{F})\to\mathcal{D}^{k}_{W}
in the previous statement is the unique linear map satisfying

\begin{cases}\iota(\rho)(f)=\rho(f),\quad&\text{ if }f\in C^{\infty}(M),\\
\iota(\rho)(\alpha)=0,\quad&\text{ if }\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}.\end{cases}

Proof.

It follows that

and that

∎

1.5.
Morphisms and extensions of relative derivations

In this section, we discuss morphisms of relative derivations, extensions and what it means to “compose” two relative degree 1 derivations.

Definition 1.29
.

Let
(\varphi,p)\colon(W,{\overline{\nabla}}^{M})\to(V,{\overline{\nabla}}^{N})
be a map of flat foliated vector bundles covering
p\colon(M,\mathcal{F}_{M})\to(N,\mathcal{F}_{N})
. We say that a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{W}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}}^{M})})
is
(\varphi,p)
-related
to a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{V}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}}^{N})})
if

(1.6)

{\mathrm{D}}_{W}\circ\varphi^{*}=\varphi^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{V}.

Note that (
1.6
) implies that the anchors of
{\mathrm{D}}_{W}
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{V}
are related by

\rho_{V}\circ\wedge^{k}\varphi={\mathrm{d}}p\circ\rho_{W}.

The relation between the associated
k
-brackets is more complicated to express since, in general, there is no map relating sections of
W
and sections of
V
. However, when
\varphi
is a fiberwise isomorphism, as in Remark
1.15
, one can express relation (
1.6
) in terms of
k
-brackets as

[\varphi^{*}v_{0},\dots,\varphi^{*}v_{k}]_{W}=\varphi^{*}[v_{0},\dots,v_{k}]_{V},\quad\text{ for all }v_{i}\in\Gamma_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}}^{V})}.

Actually, when
\varphi
is a fiberwise isomorphism, it induces a bundle map

\varphi_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}}^{W})}\to\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}}^{V})}

as follows. Recall that an element
{\mathrm{D}}_{x}
in the fiber
(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}}^{W})})_{x}
can be regarded as a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{x}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}}^{W})}\to\big(\wedge^{\bullet+k}W^{*}\big)_{x}
relative to the inclusion (as a map of foliated vector bundles). We define
\varphi_{*}({\mathrm{D}}_{x})
by

\varphi_{*}({\mathrm{D}}_{x}):=\varphi_{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{x}\circ\varphi^{*}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}}^{V})}\to\left(\wedge^{\bullet+k}V^{*}\right)_{p(x)},

where
\varphi_{*}=(\varphi^{*})^{-1}\colon\big(\wedge^{\bullet}W^{*}\big)_{x}\to\big(\wedge^{\bullet}V^{*}\big)_{p(x)}
.
Then, a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{W}
is
(\varphi,p)
-related to a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{V}
if and only if the outside square in the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,{\overline{\nabla}}^{W})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}}^{V})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{W}}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{V}}

Example 1.30

(Extensions of relative derivations)
.

If
{\mathrm{D}}_{W}
is
(\varphi,p)
-related to
{\mathrm{D}}
and the base map
p
is a submersion,
we say that
{\mathrm{D}}_{W}
is an
extension
of
{\mathrm{D}}_{V}
. In particular, we have the following:

(1)

An extension of a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}})})
to a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{V})
is an ordinary derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}
that agrees with
{\mathrm{D}}
on the flat forms:

{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\alpha={\mathrm{D}}\alpha,\quad\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}})}.

This fits into the setting of Definition
1.29
by viewing the identity map
\operatorname{id}\colon(N,0_{N})\to(N,\mathcal{F})
as a map of foliated manifolds.

(2)

More generally, given a submersion
p\colon M\to N
,
an extension of a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}})})
to a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{p})
, is a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}
on
V
relative to
p\colon M\to N

satisfying:

{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\alpha=p^{*}({\mathrm{D}}\alpha),\quad\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}})}.

Extensions will be important in the theory of relative algebroids, developed in Section
2
.

Given two ordinary derivations, their composition usually fails to be a derivation. An important exception occurs in the case of a 1-derivation
{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V})
: the square
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}:={\mathrm{D}}\circ{\mathrm{D}}
is a 2-derivation. It is easy to see that if
{\mathrm{D}}
has symbol
\rho
and associated 1-bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
then
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}
has symbol

\rho_{{\mathrm{D}}^{2}}(v_{1},v_{2})(f)=\rho(v_{1})\big(\rho(v_{2})(f)\big)-\rho(v_{2})\big(\rho(v_{1})(f)\big)-\rho([v_{1},v_{2}])(f),

while the associated 2-bracket is the Jacobiator

[v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}]_{{\mathrm{D}}^{2}}=[v_{1},[v_{2},v_{3}]]+[v_{2},[v_{3},v_{1}]]+[v_{3},[v_{1},v_{2}]].

This generalizes to relative 1-derivations which are extensions, as in Example
1.30
. For the statement, note that under the assumptions of that example, the foliation
\mathcal{F}
pulls back to a foliation
p^{*}\mathcal{F}
on
M
, and the bundle
p^{*}V
inherits a flat
p^{*}\mathcal{F}
-connection
p^{*}{\overline{\nabla}}
from
(V,\overline{\nabla})
. Pullback gives a canonical isomorphism between the space of flat forms

\Omega^{\bullet}_{(p^{*}V,\overline{\nabla})}\cong p^{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{(V,\overline{\nabla})}.

Lemma 1.31
.

If a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(p^{*}V,{\overline{\nabla}})})
extends a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{0}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}})})
, their composition

{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{0}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{(p^{*}V,{\overline{\nabla}})}\to\Omega^{\bullet+2}(p^{*}V),

is a relative 2-derivation with symbol and 2-bracket given by

(1.7)

\displaystyle\rho_{{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{0}}(p^{*}v_{1},p^{*}v_{2})(f)

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}(p^{*}v_{1})\big(\rho_{0}(v_{2})(f)\big)-\rho_{1}(p^{*}v_{2})\big(\rho_{0}(v_{1})(f)\big)

\displaystyle-\rho_{1}(p^{*}[v_{1},v_{2}]_{0})(f),

\displaystyle[p^{*}v_{1},p^{*}v_{2},p^{*}v_{3}]_{{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{0}}

\displaystyle=[v_{1},[v_{2},v_{3}]_{0}]_{1}+[v_{2},[v_{3},v_{1}]_{0}]_{1}+[v_{3},[v_{1},v_{2}]_{0}]_{1},

for any
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in\Gamma_{(V,{\overline{\nabla}})}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
.

Proof.

The fact that
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{0}
is a
2
-derivation follows from

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{0}(\alpha\wedge\beta)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{D}}_{1}({\mathrm{D}}_{0}\alpha\wedge\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\wedge{\mathrm{D}}_{0}\beta)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{D}}_{1}({\mathrm{D}}_{0}\alpha)\wedge p^{*}\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|+1}p^{*}{\mathrm{D}}_{0}\alpha\wedge{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\beta+

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(-1)^{|\alpha|}{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\alpha\wedge p^{*}{\mathrm{D}}_{0}\beta+(-1)^{2|\alpha|}p^{*}\alpha\wedge{\mathrm{D}}_{1}({\mathrm{D}}_{0}\beta)

\displaystyle=({\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{0})(\alpha)\wedge p^{*}\beta+p^{*}\alpha\wedge({\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{0})(\beta),

where we used the extension property
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\alpha=p^{*}({\mathrm{D}}_{0}\alpha)
to cancel two terms. The expressions for the symbol and 2-bracket follow from straightforward computations using the formulas for the duality (see the proof of Lemma
1.12
).
∎

1.6.
Tableaux of derivations

Tableaux are extremely useful gadgets in the theory of PDEs
[
9
,
8
,
30
]
. They codify higher order consequences of a set of partial differential equations and, under the condition of involutivity, provide a measure of the size of the space of local solutions.

Let
W,V
be finite-dimensional vector spaces. Classically, a
tableau
is a subspace
\mathcal{T}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(W,V)
or, more generally, a linear map
\tau\colon\mathcal{T}\to\operatorname{Hom}(W,V)
. In practice, a PDE does not come with a single tableau but with a family of tableaux depending on coordinates, i.e., a vector bundle. The theory of tableaux is pointwise in nature, and thus naturally carries over to vector bundles (possibly under some extra constant rank assumption). Associated to a classical tableau, one has the notions of prolongation, the Spencer complex, involutivity and of Cartan characters. We refer to
[
8
,
9
,
30
]
for more details.

It turns out that tableaux associated to classification problems as in the Introduction (or the applications of Theorem 4 in Bryant
[
8
]
) are not tableaux in the classical sense. Bryant was certainly aware of this, as is evident from his computations in
[
8
]
of the Cartan characters and prolongations. However, to the authors’ knowledge, this is not formalized or mentioned anywhere in the literature.
In this section, we will extend the classical notion of tableau by formalizing the notion of a
tableau of derivations
.

1.6.1.
Definition of tableau of derivations

In what follows, we fix vector bundles
W\to M
and
V\to N
and a vector bundle map
(\varphi,p)\colon W\to V
.

Definition 1.32
.

A
tableau of
k
-derivations
relative to a vector bundle map
\varphi
is a vector subbundle
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
.

Remark 1.33

(Tableaux for derivations relative to foliations)
.

All the definitions and results that follow apply equally well to bundles
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
of derivations relative to foliations. This is due to the pointwise nature of the operations and notions related to tableaux and the fact that
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(W,\overline{\nabla})}
is locally isomorphic to a space of derivations relative to a bundle map.

The theory of prolongations and involutivity of tableaux of derivations, rests upon the following definition.

Definition 1.34
.

The
Spencer differential

\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi})\to\mathcal{D}^{k+l}_{\varphi}
is the unique
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear map such that

\delta(\omega\otimes{\mathrm{D}})=\omega\wedge{\mathrm{D}},

for any
\omega\in\Omega^{l}(W)
and
{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi})
.

Remark 1.35
.

Sometimes, instead of a subbundle
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
, we need to consider a vector bundle map
\tau\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
that is not necessarily injective. For such a bundle map, the Spencer differential
\delta_{\tau}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{D}^{k+l}_{\varphi}
is defined by requiring

\delta_{\tau}(\omega\otimes{\mathrm{D}})=\omega\wedge\tau({\mathrm{D}}),

for
\omega\otimes{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{l}W^{*}\otimes\mathcal{T})
. For a classical tableau, such objects were considered in
[
30
]
under the name “generalized tableaux”. In this paper, we will refer to the bundle map
\tau
as a
tableau map
.

An element in
\Gamma\left(\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi})\right)
can be viewed as a
W
-form of degree
l
with values in
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
, so it is determined by its action on
\Omega^{1}(V)
. For
\xi\in\Gamma\left(\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi})\right)
, the Spencer differential
\delta\xi
is the
(k+l)
-derivation relative to
\varphi
acting on
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(V)
as

(1.8)

\begin{split}\left(\delta\xi\right)&(\alpha)(w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l+1})=\\
&=\frac{1}{(k+1)!l!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k+l+1}}(-1)^{\sigma}\left(\xi(\alpha)(w_{\sigma(1)},\dots,w_{\sigma(l)})\right)(w_{\sigma(l+1)},\dots,w_{\sigma(l+k+1)})\end{split}

where
w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l+1}\in\Gamma(W)
. Its symbol acts on a function
f\in C^{\infty}(N)
by

(1.9)

\begin{split}&\left(\delta\xi\right)(f)(w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l})\\
&=\frac{1}{k!l!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k+l}}(-1)^{\sigma}\left(\xi(f)(w_{\sigma(1)},\dots,w_{\sigma(l)})\right)(w_{\sigma(l+1)},\dots,w_{\sigma(l+k)})\end{split}

Example 1.36
.

There is more than one way of interpreting a classical tableau bundle as a tableau of derivations.

One direct way is through 0-derivations. Let
\varphi\colon W\to V
be any bundle map covering the identity on
M
(e.g., it can be the zero map). From the short exact sequence (
1.3
), it follows that
\operatorname{Hom}(W,V)\subset\mathcal{D}^{0}_{\varphi}
. Hence, a classical tableau bundle
\mathcal{T}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(W,V)
can be seen as a 0-tableau in
\mathcal{D}^{0}_{\varphi}
.

Another interpretation of a classical tableau as a bundle of derivations is through relative 1-derivations. Assume that the bundle map
(\varphi,p)\colon W\to V
is a fiberwise isomorphism. By Lemma
1.27
, there is a short exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\ker({\mathrm{d}}p))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.44803pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 133.44803pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 162.33293pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi_{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 180.18417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 180.18417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 234.39603pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 234.39603pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

A subbundle
\mathcal{T}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(W,\ker({\mathrm{d}}p))
is both a tableau in the classical sense as well as a tableau of 1-derivations.

In both cases, the Spencer differential on
\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T})
, interpreted as a tableau of derivations, corresponds to the classical Spencer differential for
\mathcal{T}
as a classical tableau. Therefore, both interpretations of
\mathcal{T}
as a tableau of derivations recover the usual prolongation and Spencer cohomology theory.

Remark 1.37
.

There is a warning: while naïvely the subbundle
\mathcal{T}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,V)
is a classical tableau (in the sense that it is a subbundle of a
\operatorname{Hom}
-space), it really depends on the context whether it should be treated as such. Usually, the exterior power
\wedge^{2}W
indicates the presence of brackets. Interpreting
\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,V)
as a classical tableau leads to different Cartan characters and prolongations. For instance, to compute the characters of
\mathcal{T}
as a classical tableau, a flag of
\wedge^{2}W
must be used. However, as we shall see, in the derivation picture one only requires a flag of
W
.

1.6.2.
Prolongations and Spencer cohomology

Given a tableau of derivations we define its prolongation as follows.

Definition 1.38
.

The
first prolongation
of a tableau
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
is defined as

\mathcal{T}^{(1)}=\ker\delta\big|_{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T})}.

The prolongation of a tableau
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
is a subbundle
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T})
whenever it has constant rank. Hence, in this case,
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
is a classical tableau and one defines the higher prolongations of
\mathcal{T}
, when they exist, recursively as

\mathcal{T}^{(m)}:=(\mathcal{T}^{(m-1)})^{(1)}.

The Spencer differentials

\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m)})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l+1}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m-1)})

are defined for
m\geq 1
as in Definition
1.34
by restricting to
\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m)})
(i.e., regarding
\mathcal{T}^{(m)}
as classical tableau for
m\geq 1
, as in Example
1.36
). Together they form a sequence of complexes

(1.10)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{(m)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T}^{(m-1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{m}W,\mathcal{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k+m}_{\varphi}.}

If necessary, we extend this complex with the zero differential.

Lemma 1.39
.

\delta^{2}=0
.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m)})
. If
m\geq 2
, then both steps in the sequence correspond to the classical Spencer differential so
\delta^{2}\xi=0
. When
m=1
, some care needs to be taken. The essential part is that
\xi
takes values in
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
, which by definition is the kernel of
\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{D}^{k+1}_{\varphi}
. The argument goes as follows. Let
w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l+1}\in W
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(V)
. We use the following notation:

•

for a natural number
n
we set
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
, remembering the order.

•

if
J\subset[n]
is an ordered subset of length
L
, we set

w_{J}:=w_{j_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge w_{j_{L}},\quad\mbox{ for $j_{1}<\dots<j_{L}\in J$}.

Then, according to Equation (
1.8
):

\displaystyle(\delta^{2}\xi)(\alpha)(w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l+1})

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{l}(-1)^{i}(-1)^{\sigma}\xi(w_{\sigma\left([l]\setminus{i}\right)})(w_{\sigma(i)})(\alpha)(w_{\sigma\left([k+l+1]\setminus[l]\right)})

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\xi(w_{\sigma([l-1])})(w_{\sigma(l)})(\alpha)(w_{\sigma\left([k+l+1]\setminus[l]\right)})

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset[k+l+1]\\
\text{s.t. }|J|=l-1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\text{ s.t.}\\
\sigma([l-1])=J\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sigma}\xi(w_{J})(w_{\sigma(l)})(\alpha)(w_{\sigma\left([k+l+1]\setminus[l]\right)})

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset[k+l+1]\\
\text{s.t. }|J|=l-1\end{subarray}}\delta\left(\xi(w_{J})\right)(\alpha)(w_{[k+l+1]\setminus J})

which vanishes because
\xi(w_{J})\in\mathcal{T}^{(1)}=\ker\delta
.
∎

Definition 1.40
.

The
Spencer cohomologies
of a tableau
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
are the cohomology groups of the complexes in Equation (
1.10
). Concretely,

\displaystyle H^{m,l}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\ker\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m)})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l+1}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m-1)})}{\operatorname{im}\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l-1}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m+1)})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}^{(m)})},\mbox{ for $m\geq 1$},

\displaystyle H^{0,l}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\ker\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{D}^{k+l}_{\varphi}}{\operatorname{im}\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l-1}W,\mathcal{T}^{(1)})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T})},

\displaystyle H^{-1,l}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathcal{D}^{k+l-1}_{\varphi}}{\operatorname{im}\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l-1}W,\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{D}^{k+l-1}_{\varphi}},\quad\mbox{for $l\geq 1$}.

Remark 1.41
.

Note that
H^{m,0}(\mathcal{T})=0
when
m\geq 1
and
H^{m,1}(\mathcal{T})=0
for
m\geq 0
, from the definition of the prolongation.

Remark 1.42
.

The index
m
in the Spencer cohomology groups of Definition
1.40
tracks the
prolongation order
of the structure, which is typical in the theory of
G
-structures (e.g.
[
21
]
). In the classical literature on formal PDEs and Spencer cohomology, the index
m
tracks the order of the PDE. The difference is a shift by one: if
\mathcal{T}
is a classical tableau, then
H^{m,l}(\mathcal{T})
from Definition
1.40
corresponds to
H^{m+1,l}(\mathcal{T})
in the classical literature on formal PDEs (e.g.
[
9
]
).

Definition 1.43
.

A tableau
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
is called
involutive
if all prolongations
\mathcal{T}^{(m)}
,
m\geq 1
, have constant rank and

H^{m,l}(\mathcal{T})=0,\quad\mbox{ for all $m\geq 0,l\geq 1$}.

Remark 1.44
.

Under the assumption that
H^{m,l}(\mathcal{T})=0
for all
m\geq 0,l\geq 1
, the condition that
\mathcal{T}^{(m)}
has constant rank for
m\geq 1
is equivalent to the Spencer cohomology groups
H^{-1,l}(\mathcal{T})
having constant rank for
l\geq 2
, which follows from the Spencer complex (
1.10
).

Moreover, since
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
is a classical tableau, the stabilization result by Serre (Appendix of
[
21
]
) implies that there exists
m_{0}
such that
H^{m,l}(\mathcal{T})=0
for
m\geq m_{0}
and
l\geq 1
. Thus, only finitely many conditions are needed to verify involutivity of a tableau of derivations.

1.6.3.
Cartan characters and Cartan’s test

Heuristically, a system of differential equations is in
involution
when there are no higher order hidden consequences of the equations. These higher order consequences appear as cohomology classes in the Spencer cohomology groups which, in general, are hard to compute. A practical way of checking involutivity is through Cartan’s test. In this section, we make these notions precise for tableaux of derivations.

We continue to assume that
(\varphi,p)\colon W\to V
is a fixed morphism of vector bundles. By a
flag
of
W
we mean a sequence of vector subbundles of
W
,

0=W_{0}\subset W_{1}\subset\dots\subset W_{n}=W,\quad\text{ with }\operatorname{rank}W_{i}=i.

Fix a flag
(W_{i})
for
W
, denote by
\iota_{i}\colon W_{i}\hookrightarrow W
the inclusion map and
\iota_{i}^{*}\colon\wedge^{\bullet}W^{*}\to\wedge^{\bullet}W_{i}^{*}
the induced restrictions. A relative
k
-derivation
{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(V)\to\Omega^{\bullet+k}(W)
can be post-composed with the restriction map to yield a
k
-derivation
\iota_{i}^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}
relative to
\varphi\circ\iota_{i}
(cf. Example
1.7
). In other words, there is restriction map

(\iota_{i})_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}\to\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi\circ\iota_{i}}.

If
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
is a tableau, we set

\mathcal{T}_{i}:=\ker(\iota_{i})_{*}\cap\mathcal{T}.

Notice that
\mathcal{T}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{i-1}
and that
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}=0
where
n=\operatorname{rank}W
.

Definition 1.45
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
be a tableau and
(W_{i})
a flag for
W
. The
Cartan characters
of
\mathcal{T}
with respect to the flag are

s_{i}:=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}_{i-1}-\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}_{i},\quad(i=1,\dots,n=\operatorname{rank}W).

As for classical tableaux, the Cartan characters bound the rank of the prolongation and, moreover, provide a practical way to verify that a tableau of derivations is involutive through Cartan’s test. Here we present an extension of Cartan’s test to tableaux of derivations.

Theorem 1.46
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
be a tableau of derivations.

(i)

(Generalized Cartan’s bound).
If
\{s_{i}\}
are the Cartan characters w.r.t. a flag
(W_{i})
, the dimension of the prolongation is constrained by

\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(1)}\leq s_{1}+2s_{2}+\dots+ns_{n}.

(ii)

(Generalized Cartan’s test).
If the Cartan characters
s_{i}
are locally constant and Cartan’s bound is achieved, i.e.,

\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(1)}=s_{1}+2s_{2}+\dots+ns_{n},

then
\mathcal{T}
is involutive.

A flag for which Cartan’s test holds is called a
regular flag
for
\mathcal{T}
. From the proof below, it will be clear that if
(W_{i})
is a regular flag for
\mathcal{T}
, then it is also a regular flag for the first prolongation
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
.

Remark 1.47
.

For a classical tableau, Cartan’s test provides an equivalent characterization of involutivity (see
[
31
]
, Theorem 3.4). We suspect that it is also an equivalence for tableaux of derivations, but it does not seem to follow from Cartan’s test applied to the prolongation as a classical tableau. The reason is that if
\mathcal{T}
is a tableau, regular flags for the prolongation
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
may not be regular for
\mathcal{T}
itself. This poses no obstacle to our applications: computing the Cartan characters is typically much more practical than computing the Spencer cohomology groups, making the implication in Theorem
1.46
the most relevant one.

Before we give the proof of the previous theorem, we make the following observation. Since the first prolongation
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T})
is also a tableau, it comes with spaces
(\mathcal{T}^{(1)})_{i}=\ker\iota_{i}^{*}\big|_{\mathcal{T}^{(1)}}
, associated to a choice of flag for
W
, where now
\iota_{i}^{*}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T})\to\operatorname{Hom}(W_{i},\mathcal{T})
is the restriction map. These are related to the prolongations of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
as follows.

Lemma 1.48
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
be a tableau of derivations and fix a flag
(W_{i})
for
W
. Then:

\left(\mathcal{T}^{(1)}\right)_{i}\subseteq(\mathcal{T}_{i})^{(1)},\quad(i=1,\dots,n).

Proof.

Let
\xi\in(\mathcal{T}^{(1)})_{i}
, so
\xi(w)=0
for all
w\in W_{i}
. Then, for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(V)
,
u\in W
and
w_{0},\dots,w_{k}\in W_{i}
, applying (
1.8
) one finds

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\delta\xi)(\alpha)(u,w_{0},\dots,w_{k})

\displaystyle=\left(\xi(u)\alpha\right)(w_{0},\dots,w_{k})-\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{j}\left(\xi(w_{j})\alpha\right)(u,w_{0},\dots,\widehat{w_{i}},\dots,w_{k})

\displaystyle=\left(\xi(u)\alpha\right)(w_{0},\dots,w_{k}).

Therefore,
\xi(u)\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
for all
u\in W
, and so
\xi\in\left(\mathcal{T}_{i}\right)^{(1)}
.
∎

Remark 1.49
.

For a tableau
\mathcal{T}\subset\operatorname{Hom}(W,V)
in the classical sense, the inclusion in Lemma
1.48
is an equality. However, in our more general case this may fail. Consider, for example, the case where
W=V
is a 2-dimensional vector space, with flag
W_{0}\subset W_{1}\subset W_{2}
, and let

\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{D}^{1}_{\varphi}=\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,W).

Then
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,W)=\mathcal{T}
, and so
