(\mathcal{T}_{1})^{(1)}=\mathcal{T}^{(1)}=\operatorname{Hom}(W,\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,W))
. On the other hand,
(\mathcal{T}^{(1)})_{1}=\operatorname{Hom}(W_{1},\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,W))\subsetneq(\mathcal{T}_{1})^{(1)}
.

The proof of Theorem
1.46
rests on the following lemma.

Lemma 1.50
.

If
L:=\ker\left(\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{D}^{k+l}_{\varphi}\right)
,
then

(1.11)

\operatorname{rank}L\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\left(\binom{n}{l}-\binom{n-i}{l}\right)

for any flag
(W_{i})
of
W
.

Proof.

Let
(W_{i})
be a flag of
W
and set

L_{i}:=L\cap\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}_{i}).

These define a filtration
L=L_{0}\supseteq\dots\supseteq L_{i-1}\supseteq L_{i}\supseteq\dots\supseteq L_{n}=0
, and there are natural inclusions

L_{i-1}/L_{i}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}\left(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}_{i-1}/\mathcal{T}_{i}\right).

If
\pi_{i}\colon W\to W/W_{i}
is the projection, then pullback gives another set of inclusions

\pi_{i}^{*}\colon\operatorname{Hom}\left(\wedge^{l}(W/W_{i}),\mathcal{T}_{i-1}/\mathcal{T}_{i}\right)\to\operatorname{Hom}\left(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}_{i-1}/\mathcal{T}_{i}\right).

Claim
.

\left(L_{i-1}/L_{i}\right)\cap\operatorname{im}\pi_{i}^{*}=\{0\}
.

To prove this claim let
\pi_{i}^{*}\xi\in L_{i-1}\cap\pi_{i}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}\left(\wedge^{l}\left(W/W_{i}\right),\mathcal{T}_{i-1}\right)
. If
w_{1},\dots w_{l}\in W
and
w_{l+1},\dots,w_{k+l+1}\in W_{i}
, then by (
1.8
) for
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(V)
we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\pi_{i}^{*}\xi\right)(\alpha)(w_{1},\dots,w_{k+l+1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{l!(k+1)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k+l+1}}(-1)^{\sigma}(\xi(\alpha)\left(\pi_{i}\left(w_{\sigma(1)}\right),\dots\pi_{i}\left(w_{\sigma(l)}\right)\right)\left(w_{\sigma(l+1)},\dots,w_{\sigma(k+l)}\right)

\displaystyle=((\pi_{i}^{*}\xi)(\alpha)(w_{1},\dots,w_{l}))(w_{l+1},\dots,w_{k+l+1}).

It follows that
\pi_{i}^{*}\xi\in\operatorname{Hom}\left(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}_{i}\right)
, and the claim follows.

The estimate of the lemma now follows from this claim by telescoping:

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}L

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{rank}L_{i-1}-\operatorname{rank}L_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T}_{i-1}/\mathcal{T}_{i})-\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}(W/W_{i}),\mathcal{T}_{i-1}/\mathcal{T}_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\left(\binom{n}{l}-\binom{n-i}{l}\right).\qed

Proof of Theorem
1.46
.

Note that the case
l=1
in Lemma
1.50
is exactly item (i) (Cartan’s bound) in the theorem.

For the proof of item (ii), suppose that
(W_{i})
is a regular flag for
\mathcal{T}
, with characters
(s_{i})
, so that Cartan’s test on
\dim\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
is satisfied. Since the Cartan characters are assumed locally constant, so is the rank of
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
.

The proof goes in two steps:

Step 1:

Prove the inequality

(1.12)

\operatorname{rank}(\operatorname{im}\delta)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\left(\binom{n}{l}-\binom{n-i}{l}\right)

for
l=2
and that Cartan’s bound is achieved for
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(2)}
– as the first prolongation of
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
– for the same flag
(W_{i})
. Then, by Lemma
1.50
, it follows that

H^{0,2}(\mathcal{T})=0,

and that – by Cartan’s test for ordinary tableaux (
[
31
, Theorem 3.4]
) – all prolongations
\mathcal{T}^{(m)}
,
m\geq 1
, have locally constant rank and are involutive, so that

H^{m,l}(\mathcal{T})=H^{m-1,l}(\mathcal{T}^{(1)})=0,\quad\text{for }m\geq 1,\,l\geq 0;

Step 2:

Prove inequality (
1.12
) for arbitrary
l
so that, applying Lemma
1.50
, one has

H^{0,l}(\mathcal{T})=0,\quad\text{for }l\geq 0.

Step 1.
First, we claim that the Cartan characters
s^{(1)}_{i}
of the first prolongation satisfy the bound:

(1.13)

s^{(1)}_{i}\leq s_{i}+s_{i+1}+\dots+s_{n}.

This follows from the Euler characteristic of the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(\mathcal{T}^{(1)}\right)_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(\mathcal{T}^{(1)}\right)_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{w_{i}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{i-1}}

where
\iota_{w_{i}}
is interior contraction by a local frame
(w_{i})
adapted to
(W_{i})
, i.e.,
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{i}\}
is a frame for
W_{i}
.

From the bound on
s_{i}^{(1)}
together with Cartan’s bound for
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(2)}
, we see that

\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(2)}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}is^{(1)}_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{i+1}{2}s_{i}.

Furthermore, from the exact row of the Spencer complex

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{(2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.45837pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.45837pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T}^{(1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 149.2592pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 165.97241pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 165.97241pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,\mathcal{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 254.9031pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 254.9031pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k+2}_{\varphi}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

we conclude that

(1.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{rank}(\operatorname{im}\delta)&=\operatorname{rank}\left(\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T}^{(1)})\right)-\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(2)}\\
&\geq\sum_{i=0}^{n}s_{i}\left(ni-\binom{i+1}{2}\right)\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\left(\binom{n}{2}-\binom{n-i}{2}\right),\end{split}

where we used the assumption that Cartan’s bound is achieved for
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
. This proves the inequality (
1.12
) for
l=2
. But then by Lemma
1.50
, the inequality (
1.14
) must be an equality, so that

\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(2)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\binom{i+1}{2}.

Hence, Cartan’s bound is achieved for
\mathcal{T}^{(2)}
and the proof of step 1 is concluded. Incidentally, this also shows that one must have equality in (
1.13
).

Step 2.
It remains to prove (
1.12
) for
l\geq 3
. This bound comes again from the Spencer complex:

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{(l)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.04172pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 76.04172pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{T}^{(l-1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 169.81967pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 169.81967pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}W,\mathcal{T}^{(l-2)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 274.75043pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 274.75043pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\dots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\\
\qquad\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l-1}W,\mathcal{T}^{(1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 118.86339pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 135.68076pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 135.68076pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}W,\mathcal{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 223.19476pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 223.19476pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k+l}_{\varphi}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

Because
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
is involutive, this sequence is exact up until
\operatorname{im}\delta
. It follows that

\operatorname{rank}(\operatorname{im}\delta)=\sum_{k=1}^{l}(-1)^{k+1}\operatorname{rank}\left(\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l-k}W,\mathcal{T}^{(k)})\right).

We already know that Cartan’s bound is achieved for all prolongations, so
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(k)}
can be given entirely in terms of the Cartan characters of
\mathcal{T}
. An induction argument using
s^{(1)}_{i}=s_{i}+\dots+s_{n}
gives

\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(k)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\binom{i+k-1}{k}
,
and therefore

\operatorname{rank}(\operatorname{im}\delta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{l}(-1)^{k+1}\binom{n}{l-k}\binom{i+k-1}{k}\right).

The result now follows from the combinatorial identity

\sum_{k=1}^{l}(-1)^{k+1}\binom{n}{l-k}\binom{i+k-1}{k}=\binom{n}{l}-\binom{n-i}{l}.\qed

1.6.4.
Symbol exact sequences of tableaux and involutivity

We continue to assume that
(\varphi,p)\colon W\to V
is a vector bundle map.

Proposition 1.51
.

Both
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,p^{*}V)\subset\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
are involutive tableaux for which every flag is regular.

In order to prove this proposition, we will use the following lemma which relates the Spencer differential and
the short exact sequence (
1.3
) induced by the symbol map.

Lemma 1.52
.

The following diagram of short exact sequences commutes

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,p^{*}V))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(W,\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,p^{*}TN))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+2}W,p^{*}V)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k+1}_{\varphi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,p^{*}TN)},}

where the side vertical arrows are skew-symmetrization. In particular, the Spencer differential
\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(W,\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi})\to\mathcal{D}^{k+1}_{\varphi}
is surjective.

Proof.

This is clear from (
1.8
) and (
1.9
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.51
.

Consider first the tableau
\mathcal{T}:=\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,p^{*}V)
. In this case the Spencer differential is the skew-symmetrization map

\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(W,\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,p^{*}V))\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,p^{*}V)

which is surjective. Setting
n=\operatorname{rank}W
and
m=\operatorname{rank}V
, we have

\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(1)}=m\left({n\binom{n}{k}-\binom{n}{k+1}}\right)=m\,k\binom{n+1}{k+1}.

Now let
(W_{i})
be a (local) flag for
W
. From the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,p^{*}V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W_{i},p^{*}V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

it follows that
s_{i}=m\left(\binom{i}{k}-\binom{i-1}{k}\right)=m\binom{i-1}{k-1}
. Hence, we find that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}is_{i}=mk\sum_{i=k}^{n}\binom{i}{k}=m\,k\binom{n+1}{k+1}=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{T}^{(1)},

so Cartan’s Test
1.46
holds and
\mathcal{T}
is involutive.

Next, to show that
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
is also involutive we apply Lemma
1.52
. Setting
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}
and
\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,p^{*}TN)
, the lemma gives a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Moreover, restriction gives a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W,p^{*}V)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W,p^{*}TN)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}W_{i},p^{*}V)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{\varphi\circ\iota_{W_{i}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}W_{i},p^{*}TN)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

so we obtain that the restricted spaces also fit into a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since Cartan’s test is satisfied for both
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
, we conclude that it is satisfied for
\mathcal{D}
, which then must also be involutive.
∎

2.
Relative algebroids

2.1.
Relative algebroids

With the formalism of relative derivations at hand, we are now ready to introduce the geometric objects underlying Cartan’s realization problem and Bryant’s equations (
0.5
).

Definition 2.1
.

An
almost Lie algebroid relative to a submersion
or in short
algebroid relative to a submersion

p\colon M\to N
consists of a vector bundle
A\to N
together with a 1-derivation

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(p^{*}A)

relative to
p
.

We will denote by
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
the entire structure of the algebroid relative to
p
and often write
B
for the pullback bundle
p^{*}A\to M
. The manifolds
M
and
N
will be specified when those are not clear from the context. We like to graphically depict an algebroid relative to
p
as

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{N}

where the dotted arrow is not a map but indicates a derivation that is defined on
\Omega^{\bullet}(A)
and takes values in
\Omega^{\bullet+1}(B)
. We use the letter
B
to stand for Bryant, whose equations (
0.5
) inspired this definition. This justifies in part the change of notation from the previous section, where the bundles
A
and
B
were denoted by
V
and
W
, respectively.

According to Lemma
1.12
, the structure of an algebroid relative to
p\colon M\to N
can also be encoded by a relative bracket and anchor

[\cdot,\cdot]\colon\wedge^{2}\Gamma(A)\to\Gamma(B),\quad\rho\colon B\to p^{*}TN

subject to the Leibniz rule:

[a_{0},fa_{1}]=p^{*}f[a_{0},a_{1}]+\mathcal{L}_{\rho(p^{*}a_{0})}(f)p^{*}a_{1}.

The corresponding derivation is determined through the Koszul formula (see the proof of Lemma
1.12
).

Example 2.2
.

In the case that
M=N
and
p=\operatorname{id}
, we recover a notion of an
almost Lie algebroid
: a vector bundle
A\to N
together with an anchor and a bracket subject to the Leibniz rule, or, dually, with a degree 1 derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{A}
on
\Omega^{\bullet}(A)
. Such an almost Lie algebroid is a Lie algebroid when
{\mathrm{D}}_{A}^{2}=0
. In particular, we can consider any (almost) Lie algebroid as an algebroid relative to the identity. A special case is the tangent bundle
(TP,{\mathrm{d}})\to P
with the de Rham differential
{\mathrm{d}}
.

At this point, when dealing with an arbitrary relative algebroid, it is not clear how to make sense of “
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
”. We will soon see how to handle this issue. This issue also influences our terminology: the objects in Definition
2.1
should properly be called
almost relative Lie algebroid
. This name is too long and so we refer to them simply as
relative algebroids
, removing “Lie” from its name rather than adding “almost”. Later, after we make sense of
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
, we will be able to define
relative Lie algebroids
.

Example 2.3

(Relative algebroids in coordinates)
.

Let
(U,(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho}))
be a coordinate system for
M
and
(V,x^{\mu})
a coordinate system for
N
such that

p(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho})=x^{\mu}.

Also, fix
\{e_{i}\}
a frame for
A|_{V}
, and let
\{\theta^{i}\}
be the corresponding dual coframe. Then the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
is determined by

(2.1)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}c^{i}_{jk}(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho})\,\theta^{j}\wedge\theta^{k},\\
{\mathrm{D}}x^{\mu}=F^{\mu}_{i}(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho})\,\theta^{i}.\end{cases}

for functions
c^{i}_{jk},F^{\mu}_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Dually, the anchor and bracket are given by

\begin{cases}[e_{i},e_{j}]=c_{ij}^{k}e_{k},\\
\rho(e_{i})=F^{\mu}_{i}\partial_{x^{\mu}}.\end{cases}

The equations above look strikingly similar to Bryant’s equations (
0.5
) except that one has
{\mathrm{D}}
instead of
{\mathrm{d}}
and the
c^{i}_{jk}
’s also depend on the “free derivatives”
y^{\varrho}
.

A true globalization of Bryant’s equations requires a globalization of the projection. The language introduced in the previous section for derivations relative to foliations precisely captures this idea. This is not a significant generalization; as we will see in Section
3
, whenever the first prolongation of a relative algebroid exists, it is always relative to a submersion.

Nevertheless, there are natural examples of relative algebroids that are relative to a foliation rather than a submersion. These include relative algebroids underlying Pfaffian fibrations
[
11
]
and those obtained through restrictions (Proposition
4.12
).

Definition 2.4
.

An
algebroid relative to a foliation

(M,\mathcal{F})
is a flat foliated vector bundle
(M,\mathcal{F},B,\overline{\nabla})
together with a 1-derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
relative to
\mathcal{F}
:

{\mathrm{D}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{B}.

We will write
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
to denote a relative algebroid over
(M,\mathcal{F})
. If there is ambiguity in what
(M,\mathcal{F})
could be, we write
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
.

Example 2.5
.

Let
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
be an algebroid relative to a submersion
p\colon M\to N
. Then it can be viewed as an algebroid relative to the foliation
\mathcal{F}=\ker({\mathrm{d}}p)
. Namely, the vector bundle
B=p^{*}A
carries a canonical flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection
{\overline{\nabla}}
determined by requiring

\overline{\nabla}p^{*}a=0,\quad\text{ for all }a\in\Gamma(A),

and since
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}=p^{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{A}
,
we can view
{\mathrm{D}}
as a derivation relative to
\mathcal{F}
.

Conversely, any algebroid relative to a foliation is locally an algebroid relative to a submersion. It is globally an algebroid relative to a submersion precisely when
\mathcal{F}
is simple and
\overline{\nabla}
has no holonomy along
\mathcal{F}
(see Proposition
1.17
).

Suppose we choose foliation coordinates
(U,(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho}))
on
(M,\mathcal{F})
, so that plaques of
\mathcal{F}
correspond to
\{y^{\varrho}=c^{\varrho}\}
. Furthermore, let
\{e_{i}\}
be a local frame of flat sections of
B|_{U}
, so the dual coframe
\{\theta^{i}\}
consists of local flat
B
-forms in
\Omega^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}(U)
. Then the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
is still determined by the same equations (
2.1
). Also, we now see that we can retrieve Bryant’s equations, i.e., have the
c_{ij}^{k}
not depend on the free variables, exactly when there is a local coframe
(\theta^{i})
such that
\overline{\nabla}({\mathrm{D}}\theta^{i})=0
. We will see later that this property is always satisfied for any prolongation of a relative algebroid (Definition
3.1
).

Using the notion of
(\varphi,p)
-related derivations (see Definition
1.29
), morphisms of relative algebroids can be defined as follows:

Definition 2.6
.

A
morphism

(\varphi,p)\colon(B_{1},\overline{\nabla}_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})\to(B_{2},\overline{\nabla}_{2},{\mathrm{D}}_{2})
of relative algebroids consists of

(i)

a map of foliations
p\colon(M_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to(M_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})
,

(ii)

a map of flat foliated vector bundles
\varphi\colon(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}_{1})\to(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}_{2})
covering
p
,

such that
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}
is
(\varphi,p)
-related to
{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
:

{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ\varphi^{*}=\varphi^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{2},\quad\text{ on $\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}_{2})}$}.

2.2.
Realizations

In this section, we will fix an algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
relative to a foliation
(M,\mathcal{F})
.

The tangent bundle of any manifold
P
is a Lie algebroid with derivation
{\mathrm{d}}
, the de Rham differential, so we will denote it by
(TP,{\mathrm{d}})
. A realization is an object that “realizes” an algebroid as the tangent bundle of a manifold.

Definition 2.7
.

A
realization

(P,r,\theta)
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is a morphism of relative algebroids from
(TP,{\mathrm{d}})
to
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
that is fiberwise an isomorphism.

Explicitly, a realization consists of a manifold
P
together with a bundle map
(\theta,r)\colon TP\to B
that is fiberwise an isomorphism and satisfies

{\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}=\theta^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}},\quad\text{on }\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}.

Remark 2.8
.

In
[
16
,
17
]
, realizations of Lie algebroids are defined through Maurer-Cartan forms. In our situation, the bundle map
(\theta,r)
can be reinterpreted as a one-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(P;r^{*}B)
that is fiberwise an isomorphism. Choosing any extension
\nabla
of
\overline{\nabla}
on
B
, Lemma
1.26
gives a splitting under which the relative derivation
{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{*}-\theta^{*}{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(r^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})})
decomposes into two components:
(\operatorname{MC}^{\nabla}_{\theta},\Pi\circ{\mathrm{d}}r-\rho\circ\theta)
, where
\Pi\colon TM\to\nu(\mathcal{F})
is the projection and the Maurer-Cartan form is given by

\operatorname{MC}_{\theta}^{\nabla}:={\mathrm{d}}^{\nabla}\theta+\frac{1}{2}[\theta,\theta]_{\nabla}.

So we conclude that in terms of anchors and brackets a bundle map
(\theta,r)\colon TP\to B
is a realization of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
if and only if it is fiberwise an isomorphism and satisfies

(2.2)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}^{\nabla}\theta=-\frac{1}{2}[\theta,\theta]_{\nabla},\\
\Pi\circ{\mathrm{d}}r=\rho\circ\theta.\end{cases}

We conclude that:

(i)

If
\theta
is anchored (i.e.
\Pi\circ{\mathrm{d}}r=\rho\circ\theta
), then the Maurer-Cartan form is independent of
\nabla
.

(ii)

If
\theta
is anchored, it is a realization if and only if its Maurer-Cartan form vanishes.

In local coordinates (see Examples
2.3
and
2.5
), writing
\theta=(\theta^{i})\colon TP\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r=(a^{\mu},b^{\varrho})\colon P\to\mathbb{R}^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
, equations (
2.2
) become

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}c^{i}_{jk}(a,b)\,\theta^{j}\wedge\theta^{k},\\
{\mathrm{d}}a^{\mu}=F^{\mu}_{i}(a,b)\,\theta^{i},\end{cases}

and these are exactly Bryant’s equations (
0.5
) (see Example
2.5
for why the
c^{i}_{jk}
may depend on the free derivatives
b^{\varrho}
).

2.3.
Tableaux

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be an algebroid relative to a foliation
(M,\mathcal{F})
. Recall from Lemma
1.20
that there is a canonical flat
\mathcal{F}
-connection on the vector bundle
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
also denoted by
\overline{\nabla}
.

Definition 2.9
.

The
tableau map
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is the bundle map

\tau\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})},\quad X\mapsto\overline{\nabla}_{X}{\mathrm{D}}

The relative algebroid is called
standard
or
non-degenerate
when its tableau map
\tau
is fiberwise injective.

The tableau map measures the dependence of the relative algebroid structure on the directions of
\mathcal{F}
. It is a tableau map of derivations relative to foliations. By Remark
1.33
and Remark
1.35
, the theory of involutivity and Spencer cohomology carries over to this setting.

Composing a tableau map with the symbol map

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(\tau)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(B,\nu(\mathcal{F}))}

we see that our tableau map covers a classical tableau map

\sigma(\tau)\colon\mathcal{F}\to\operatorname{Hom}(B,\nu(\mathcal{F})),\quad X\mapsto\overline{\nabla}_{X}\rho

where
\rho=\sigma({\mathrm{D}})
is the anchor of the relative algebroid. We call
\sigma(\tau)
the
symbol tableau
of the relative algebroid. Explicitly, it is given by

(2.3)

\sigma(\tau)(X)(b)=\overline{\nabla}^{\mathrm{Bott}}_{X}\rho(b)-\rho\left(\overline{\nabla}_{X}b\right),\quad\text{ for $X\in\mathcal{F}$ and $b\in\Gamma(B)$}.

On the other hand, using Lemma
1.12
, one finds that the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\tau(X)}
associated to derivation
\tau(X)={\overline{\nabla}}_{X}{\mathrm{D}}
is given by

(2.4)

[b_{1},b_{2}]_{\tau(X)}={\overline{\nabla}}_{X}([b_{1},b_{2}]),\quad\text{ for }b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})},

where
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the bracket associated to
{\mathrm{D}}
.

2.4.
Torsion

We will now discuss the first order obstructions to the existence of realizations of a relative algebroid. In the following discussion we fix a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
over
(M,\mathcal{F})
, and we denote by
[\cdot,\cdot]
and
\rho
the corresponding bracket and anchor. Recall from Lemma
1.27
that there is an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

A
pointwise lift
or
completion
of
{\mathrm{D}}
at
m\in M
is an element
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\in(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B})_{m}

such that
\Pi(\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m})={\mathrm{D}}_{m}
. We let

L:=\{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\in\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B}\ |\ \Pi(\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m})={\mathrm{D}}_{m}\text{ for some $m\in M$ }\}=\Pi^{-1}({\mathrm{D}})

be the space of completions of
{\mathrm{D}}
, with projection
p_{1}\colon L\to M
,
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\mapsto m
. It is an affine bundle modeled on
\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathcal{F})
. The foliation
\mathcal{F}
pulls back to a foliation
p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{F}
on
L
, and the bundle
p_{1}^{*}B
inherits a flat
p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{F}
-connection
p_{1}^{*}\overline{\nabla}
from
(B,\overline{\nabla})
.

Remark 2.10
.

It follows from Lemma
1.27
that a pointwise lift
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}
of
{\mathrm{D}}
is completely determined by a pointwise lift
\tilde{\rho}_{m}\colon B_{m}\to T_{m}M
of the symbol
\rho
. In terms of the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]:\Gamma_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}\times\Gamma_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}\to\Gamma(B)
, this means that such a lift determines a unique extension to a bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}:\Gamma(B)\times\Gamma(B)\to B_{m}
, defined on all sections by requiring

[b_{1},fb_{2}]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}=f(m)[b_{1},b_{2}](m)+\langle\tilde{\rho}(b_{1}),{\mathrm{d}}_{m}f\rangle b_{2}(m),

for any
b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

For the next definition we recall that, according to Example
1.8
, an element
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\in L
can be regarded as a derivation
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(B)\to\wedge^{\bullet+1}B^{*}_{m}
.

Definition 2.11
.

The
torsion
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is the 2-derivation
T\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{2}_{(p_{1}^{*}B,p_{1}^{*}\overline{\nabla})})
relative to
p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{F}
defined by

T(p^{*}_{1}\alpha)\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}}:=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\left({\mathrm{D}}\alpha\right),\quad\text{for $p_{1}^{*}\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(p_{1}^{*}B,\overline{\nabla})}\cong p_{1}^{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}$.}

The symbol
\sigma(T)
is called the
symbol torsion
of
{\mathrm{D}}
.

It follows from Lemma
1.31
that the torsion
T
has associated 2-bracket given by

(2.5)

[p^{*}_{1}b_{1},p^{*}_{1}b_{2},p^{*}_{1}b_{3}]|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}}=[[b_{1},b_{2}],b_{3}]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}+[[b_{2},b_{3}],b_{1}]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}+[[b_{3},b_{1}],b_{2}]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}},

for
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\in\Gamma_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}
, where
\tilde{\rho}_{m}
and
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}
denote the symbol and the bracket of
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}
, as in Remark
2.10
. The symbol torsion, in turn, is given by

(2.6)

\sigma(T)(p^{*}b_{1},p^{*}b_{2})|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}}(f)=\tilde{\rho}_{m}(b_{1})\left(\rho(b_{2})(f)\right)-\tilde{\rho}_{m}(b_{2})\left(\rho(b_{1})(f)\right)-\rho_{m}([b_{1},b_{2}])(f),

for each
b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
and all
f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
.

Let
(P,r,\theta)
be a realization of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
. For each
p\in P
, it determines the lift of
\rho
at
r(p)
given by

(2.7)

\tilde{\rho}_{r(p)}:={\mathrm{d}}_{p}r\circ\theta_{p}\colon B_{r(p)}\to T_{r(p)}M.

Therefore, it determines also a lift
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{r(p)}
of
{\mathrm{D}}
.

Proposition 2.12

(Existence of Realizations: first order necessary condition)
.

Let
(P,r,\theta)
be a realization of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
. Then, for each
p\in P
we have

T\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{r(p)}}=0.

Proof.

By definition,
{\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}=\theta^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}
on
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
, so the derivation

\left(\theta^{-1}_{p}\right)^{*}\circ\left({\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}\right)|_{p}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(B)\to\wedge^{\bullet+1}T^{*}_{p}P\to\wedge^{\bullet+1}B^{*}_{r(p)}

is a lift of
{\mathrm{D}}
at
r(p)
. Since its symbol is (
2.7
), it must coincide with
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{r(p)}
. The proposition now follows from
{\mathrm{d}}^{2}=0
.
∎

Recall that any two pointwise lifts of
{\mathrm{D}}_{m}
differ by an element
\xi\in\operatorname{Hom}(B_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})
, where
\xi
acts as a derivation as described in the Lemma
1.27
. See Remark
1.35
for the definition of the Spencer differential
\delta_{\tau}
of the tableau map
\tau
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}
be a pointwise lift of
{\mathrm{D}}
at
m
and
\xi\in\operatorname{Hom}(B_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})
. Then

T\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}+\xi}-T\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}}=\delta_{\tau}\xi,\quad\text{ in }\Omega^{\bullet}_{(p_{1}^{*}B,\overline{\nabla})}\cong p_{1}^{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}.

Proof.

Note that
\overline{\nabla}_{X}({\mathrm{D}}\alpha)=(\overline{\nabla}_{X}{\mathrm{D}})\alpha=\tau(X)\alpha
for
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
. By Lemma
1.27
, we find that for
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
we have

\displaystyle\left(T(\beta)\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}+\xi}-T(\beta)\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}\right)(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})

\displaystyle=\iota(\xi)({\mathrm{D}}\beta)(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})

\displaystyle=\overline{\nabla}_{\xi(b_{1})}({\mathrm{D}}\beta)(b_{2},b_{3})+\mbox{c.p.}

\displaystyle=\tau(\xi(b_{1}))\beta(b_{2},b_{3})+\mbox{c.p.}

\displaystyle=\left(\delta_{\tau}\xi\right)(\beta)(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}).

Similarly, for
f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
, we find

\displaystyle\left(T(f)\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}+\xi}-T(f)\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}\right)(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{\nabla}_{\xi(b_{1})}({\mathrm{D}}f)(b_{2})-\overline{\nabla}_{\xi(b_{2})}({\mathrm{D}}f)(b_{1})

\displaystyle=\left(\delta_{\tau}\xi\right)(f)(b_{1},b_{2}),

which completes the proof.
∎

This justifies the following definition.

Definition 2.14
.

The
intrinsic torsion
of a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is the section
\Theta\in\Gamma(H^{-1,2}(\tau))
defined by

\Theta_{m}:=[T\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}}],

for any
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\in L_{m}
.

Remark 2.15
.

In the context of Section
1.6
, the torsion class lives in the Spencer cohomology of the Spencer complex (Equations (
1.10
)) of a tableau map (Remark
1.35
) of degree-1 derivations relative to a foliation (Remark
1.33
). Thus, in Definition
1.40
, we have
m=-1,k=1
, and
l=2
, and we are looking at the cohomology of the complex

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{2}_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Corollary 2.16
.

If a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
admits a realization through
m
then
\Theta_{m}=0
.

2.5.
Curvature

Suppose we are given a section
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\in\Gamma(L)
. In this case,
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
is a completion of the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
which is an actual 1-derivation on
B
, so
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}
defines a 2-derivation on
B
. Recalling again from Lemma
1.27
that there is a short exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B,\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 116.6564pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 116.6564pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{2}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 143.22688pt\raise 5.39168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Pi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.17828pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 161.17828pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{2}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 219.21411pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 219.21411pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We conclude that:

Proposition 2.17
.

Given a section
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\in\Gamma(L)
, the torsion of
{\mathrm{D}}
along
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
is

T|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}=\Pi(\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}).

If
T
vanishes identically on the image of
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
, we call
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
a
torsionless lift
of
{\mathrm{D}}
. In that case, according to the previous short exact sequence, the derivation
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}
is a section of
\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B,\mathcal{F})
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\in\Gamma(M^{(1)})
be a torsionless lift of
{\mathrm{D}}
. The
curvature of
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
is the section

\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}\in\Gamma(\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B,\mathcal{F}))\subset\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{2}_{B}).

Remark 2.19
.

\kappa_{{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}}(b_{1},b_{2})=[\tilde{\rho}(b_{1}),\tilde{\rho}(b_{2})]-\tilde{\rho}([b_{1},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}),

for
b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma(B)
.
Note that the curvature depends pointwise on the first jet of the section
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
and that for any local sections
b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma_{B}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
one has

\kappa_{{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}}(b_{1},b_{2})(f)=0.

Moreover, the fact that
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
is a torsionless lift of
{\mathrm{D}}
implies that for any local
flat
sections
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\in\Gamma_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}
one also has

(2.8)

[[b_{1},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{3}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}+[[b_{2},b_{3}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{1}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}+[[b_{3},b_{1}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}=0.

Lemma 2.20
.

The curvature
\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}
is closed in the Spencer complex of
\tau
:

\delta_{\tau}\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}=0.

Proof.

Since the curvature is uniquely determined by its symbol, it is enough to show that the Spencer differential of the symbol tableau vanishes (Section
2.3
). Since the result is a
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear, it is enough to show that it vanishes on parallel sections. For
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\in\Gamma_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
we find, combining (
1.9
) and (
2.3
),

\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma(\tau)}\sigma\left(\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}\right)(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})

\displaystyle=\overline{\nabla}^{\mathrm{Bott}}_{\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}(b_{1},b_{2})}\rho(b_{3})+\mathrm{c.p.}

\displaystyle=\Pi\left(\left[\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}(b_{1},b_{2}),\tilde{\rho}(b_{3})\right]\right)+\mathrm{c.p.}

\displaystyle=-\Pi\left(\left[\tilde{\rho}\left([b_{1},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}\right),\tilde{\rho}(b_{3})\right]\right)+\mathrm{c.p.}

\displaystyle=-\Pi\left(\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}\left([b_{1},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{3}\right)\right)-\Pi\circ\tilde{\rho}\left([[b_{1},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{3}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}\right)+\mathrm{c.p.}

\displaystyle=-\rho([[b_{1},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{3}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}+[[b_{2},b_{3}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{1}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}+[[b_{3},b_{1}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}},b_{2}]_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}})=0

where
\Pi\colon TM\to\nu(\mathcal{F})
is the projection and the last identity follows from (
2.8
).
∎

Definition 2.21
.

The
intrinsic curvature
of the relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is the section
\Omega\in\Gamma\left(p_{1}^{*}H^{0,2}(\tau)\right)
defined along a torsionless lift
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\in\Gamma(M^{(1)})
as

\Omega\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}:=[\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}].

Remark 2.22
.

The intrinsic curvature
\Omega
depends affinely on the values of
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
in
M^{(1)}
, and not on its first derivatives, in contrast to
\kappa_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}
, which depends on the first jet of
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
.

3.
Prolongation and integrability

We shall now attempt to complete a relative algebroid to a true Lie algebroid.

3.1.
Prolongations

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be an algebroid relative to a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
M
. There is no direct way to make sense of “
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}=0
”, because
{\mathrm{D}}
is only defined on flat forms in
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
. To remedy this issue we consider extensions of
{\mathrm{D}}
to
\Omega^{\bullet}_{B}
as in Section
1.5
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
prolongation
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is an algebroid
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
relative to a submersion
p_{1}\colon M_{1}\to M
satisfying

(i)

(Extension)
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\alpha=(p_{1})^{*}{\mathrm{D}}\alpha
for
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
, and

(ii)

(Completion)
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\circ{\mathrm{D}}=0
.

In this context, we write
B_{1}:=p_{1}^{*}B
for the pullback bundle.

If
(A,p,D)
is an algebroid relative to a submersion
p\colon M\to N
, we like to graphically depict a prolongation as

\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}}
\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{N.}

Remark 3.2
.

Note that for a relative algebroid the anchor and the bracket do not satisfy integrability conditions. For a prolongation, condition (ii) imposes a set of integrability conditions. By Lemma
1.31
, in terms of the brackets of
{\mathrm{D}}
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{1}
, this condition amounts to the Jacobi type identity

[b_{1},[b_{2},b_{3}]]_{1}+[b_{2},[b_{3},b_{1}]]_{1}+[b_{3},[b_{1},b_{2}]]_{1}=0,

together with the fact that the anchor almost preserves brackets:

\rho_{1}(p^{*}[b_{1},b_{2}])(f)=\rho_{1}(p^{*}b_{1})\big(\rho_{0}(b_{2})(f)\big)-\rho_{1}(p^{*}b_{2})\big(\rho_{0}(b_{1})(f)\big),

for any sections
b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{{bas}}}
.

Prolongations may or may not exist, and their existence is contingent on vanishing of the intrinsic torsion. Thus, the space of torsionless lifts

p_{1}\colon M^{(1)}\to M,\quad\mbox{where }M^{(1)}=\{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\in L\colon T\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}}=0\},

plays a special role. Actually, if this space is smooth then it yields a canonical prolongation!

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be an algebroid relative to a foliation
(M,\mathcal{F})
and assume
M^{(1)}
has a smooth structure such that
p_{1}\colon M^{(1)}\to M
is a submersion. Then the relative algebroid
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
, where
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}
is defined by

({\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\alpha)\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}}:=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\alpha,\quad\mbox{for $\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{B}$ and $\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\in M^{(1)}$,}

is a prolongation of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.

Remark 3.4
.

We will see later, in Example
4.6
, that the prolongation
M^{(1)}
is
universal
: any other prolongation must factor through
M^{(1)}
.

Proof.

Since
p_{1}
is a submersion, one checks immediately that for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{B}
,
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\alpha
is a smooth form. The derivation property then follows from the fact that each
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m}\in M^{(1)}
is a derivation relative to the inclusion. From the definition of
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}
it follows that

{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\alpha=(p_{1})^{*}{\mathrm{D}}\alpha,\quad\text{for $\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}$}.

Finally, since every
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\in M^{(1)}
is a torsionless lift of
{\mathrm{D}}
, it follows that
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\circ{\mathrm{D}}\big|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}=T_{\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}}=0
. Hence,
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
is a prolongation of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.
∎

Example 3.5

(1st prolongation in coordinates)
.

Let us assume that we have fixed local coordinates
(U,(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho}))
and a frame
\{e_{i}\}
with dual coframe
\{\theta^{i}\}
, as in Examples
2.3
and
2.5
. The derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
is determined by

(3.1)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}c^{i}_{jk}(x,y)\,\theta^{j}\wedge\theta^{k},\\
{\mathrm{D}}x^{\mu}=F^{\mu}_{i}(x,y)\,\theta^{i},\end{cases}

for some functions
c^{i}_{jk},F^{\mu}_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M)
. The completions of the anchor take the form

\tilde{\rho}(e_{i})=F^{\mu}_{i}(x,y)\partial_{x^{\mu}}+u_{i}^{\varrho}\partial_{y^{\varrho}},

where
u_{i}^{\varrho}
should be thought of as coordinates on the fibers of
L\to M
. The corresponding completion of the derivation
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
is determined by
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}y^{\varrho}=u^{\varrho}_{i}\theta^{i}
.

The first prolongation space
M^{(1)}
consists of points
(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho},u_{j}^{\varsigma})
for which
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\circ{\mathrm{D}}=0
. This yields a system of equations

\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathrm{D}}x^{\mu}=0,\qquad\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{i}=0.

The first set of equations corresponds to the vanishing of the symbol torsion associated with the extension – see (
2.6
):

\tilde{\rho}(e_{i})(\rho(e_{j})(x^{\mu})-\tilde{\rho}(e_{j})(\rho(e_{i})(x^{\mu})-\rho([e_{i},e_{j}])(x^{\mu})=0,

while the second set of equations amounts to the vanishing of the torsion on the frame
\{e_{i}\}
– see (
2.5
):

[[e_{1},e_{2}],e_{3}]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}+[[e_{2},e_{3}],e_{1}]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}+[[e_{3},e_{1}],e_{2}]_{\tilde{\rho}_{m}}=0.

Together they express the vanishing of the total torsion, and they give rise to the system of equations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F^{\mu}_{i}}{\partial y^{\varrho}}u_{j}^{\varrho}-\frac{\partial F^{\mu}_{j}}{\partial y^{\varrho}}u_{i}^{\varrho}

\displaystyle=F_{i}^{\nu}\frac{\partial F^{\mu}_{j}}{\partial x^{\nu}}-F_{j}^{\nu}\frac{\partial F^{\mu}_{i}}{\partial x^{\nu}}-c^{k}_{ij}F_{k}^{\mu},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial c^{i}_{jk}}{\partial y^{\varrho}}u^{\varrho}_{l}+\frac{\partial c^{i}_{kl}}{\partial y^{\varrho}}u^{\varrho}_{j}+\frac{\partial c^{i}_{lj}}{\partial y^{\varrho}}u^{\varrho}_{k}

\displaystyle=c^{i}_{mj}c^{m}_{kl}+c^{i}_{mk}c^{m}_{lj}+c^{i}_{ml}c^{m}_{jk}

\displaystyle\qquad-F^{\mu}_{j}\frac{\partial c^{i}_{kl}}{\partial x^{\mu}}-F^{\mu}_{k}\frac{\partial c^{i}_{lj}}{\partial x^{\mu}}-F^{\mu}_{l}\frac{\partial c^{i}_{jk}}{\partial x^{\mu}}.

Assuming that
M^{(1)}
is smooth, these equations will determine a subset of the variables
u_{i}^{\alpha}
as functions of
x^{\mu}
,
y^{\varrho}
and the remaining variables
u_{i}^{\varrho}
, call them
v^{\varphi}
. Hence, we have a set of local coordinates
(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho},v^{\varphi})
for
M^{(1)}
and the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}
is then defined by (
3.1
) together with

{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}y^{\varrho}=u^{\varrho}_{i}(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho},v^{\varphi})\,\theta^{i}.

In what follows, given a relative algebroid it will be convenient to identify the tableau of its 1st prolongation with the 1st prolongation of its tableau. The precise identification is as follows.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid. The tableau of its first prolongation is canonically isomorphic to
p_{1}^{*}\tau^{(1)}\subset p_{1}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathcal{F})
(as a tableau), where
\tau^{(1)}
is the first prolongation of the tableau
\tau
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.

Proof.

Notice that we have the following:

(a)

The 1st prolongation
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
has a fiberwise injective tableau map

\ker{\mathrm{d}}p_{1}\to p^{*}_{1}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B},\quad X\mapsto{\overline{\nabla}}_{X}{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)};

(b)

The 1st prolongation of
\tau
is the classical tableau

\tau^{(1)}:=\ker(\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathcal{F})\to\mathcal{D}^{2}_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})});

Now, the bundle
p_{1}\colon L\to M
is an affine bundle modeled on
\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathcal{F})
, and by Proposition
2.13
, the restriction
p_{1}\colon M^{(1)}\to M
is an affine bundle modeled on
\tau^{(1)}
. It follows that we have an isomorphism of tableaux

\ker{\mathrm{d}}p_{1}\simeq p^{*}_{1}\tau^{(1)}.\qed

3.2.
Integrability

We now consider the problem of existence of prolongations and integrability.

Definition 3.7
.

Given a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
:

•

its
first prolongation
is the relative algebroid
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
given by Proposition
3.3
, provided it exists;

•

its
k
-th prolongation
is defined iteratively through

(B^{(k-1)},p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}^{(k)}):=(B^{(k-2)},p_{k-1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(k-1)})^{(1)},

provided it exists.

The relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is called
k
-integrable
if all the prolongations up to and including
k
exist, and
formally integrable
if it is
k
-integrable for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If
M^{(k)}=M^{(k+1)}
for some
k
, then we say that the relative algebroid is of
finite type
, otherwise we say that it is of
infinite type
.

Our next result shows that the curvature is precisely the second order obstruction to integrability of a relative algebroid. In order to state it, note that – see also Example
1.36
:

(a)

If we view
\tau^{(1)}
as a classical tableau, then the cohomology group
H^{-1,2}(\tau^{(1)})
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B,\mathcal{F})/\operatorname{im}\delta
;

(b)

If we view
\tau^{(1)}
as a tableau of derivations, then the cohomology group
H^{-1,2}(\tau^{(1)})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{B}/\operatorname{im}\delta
. This is where the torsion class of the first prolongation lives.

(c)

The curvature class
\Omega
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
lives in
H^{0,2}(\tau)=\ker\delta/\operatorname{im}\delta
.

By Lemma
1.27
, we have an inclusion

\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B,\mathcal{F})\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{2}_{B}}

which sends
\operatorname{im}\delta
isomorphically onto
\operatorname{im}\delta
, and hence induces an inclusion in Spencer cohomology

(3.2)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.83855pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.83855pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{0,2}(\tau)\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.83855pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.83855pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.83855pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{-1,2}(\tau^{(1)})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We can state the result about 2nd order obstructions as follows.

Theorem 3.8

(Fundamental Theorem of Prolongation)
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be a 1-integrable relative algebroid. Under the inclusion map (
3.2
) the torsion class of the first prolongation coincides with the curvature class of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.

Remark 3.9
.

The
extension problem
for relative algebroids asks: given a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
, is there an extension
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
of
{\mathrm{D}}
to
\Omega^{\bullet}_{B}
such that
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}=0
? In other words, can one complete the relative algebroid to a Lie algebroid? The curvature serves as an obstruction to this extension problem.

On the other hand, we will see later (cf. Proposition
3.19
) that realizations of a relative algebroid are in a 1:1 correspondence with realizations of its first prolongation. Thus, by Proposition
2.12
, the torsion of the first prolongation is an obstruction to the existence of realizations.

Therefore, the Fundamental Theorem of Prolongation implies that, at the formal level, the extension problem is equivalent to the realization problem.

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

The statement depends only pointwise on elements in
M^{(1)}
. So, in a neighborhood of a point in
M^{(1)}
, we can assume that we have a flat Ehresmann connection, which we view as bundle map
h\colon p_{1}^{*}TM\to TM^{(1)}
satisfying

{\mathrm{d}}p_{1}(h(\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m},v))=v,\quad\text{for all }v\in T_{m}M.

The connection
h
induces a splitting of the map
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B^{(1)}}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{p_{1}^{*}}\cong p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B}
compatible with the symbol exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p_{1}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{p^{*}_{1}\operatorname{Hom}(B,TM)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B^{(1)},B^{(1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B^{(1)}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(B^{(1)},TM^{(1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

For a point in
M^{(1)}
, let
\sigma\colon M\to M^{(1)}
be the unique local flat section through that point, so that

(3.3)

h(\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{m},v)={\mathrm{d}}_{m}\sigma(v).

We denote by
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{\sigma}
the derivation of
\Omega^{\bullet}_{B}
determined by
\sigma
, so

\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{\sigma}|_{m}:=\sigma(m).

(it is convenient to keep the distinction between
\sigma
as a section and
\sigma
as a derivation). The theorem will follow from the identity:

(3.4)

\sigma^{*}\circ(h_{*}{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\circ{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{\sigma}^{2}.

Note that
h_{*}{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}
is a derivation in
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B^{(1)}}
that extends
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}
, so
h_{*}{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\circ{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}
is a 2-derivation in
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{p^{*}_{1}}\cong p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{B}
(see Section
1.5
). Precomposing with
\sigma^{*}
is the same as pulling back this 2-derivation to
\sigma^{*}p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{B}\cong\mathcal{D}^{2}_{B}
, so the equation makes sense.

Assume (
3.4
) holds. Passing to Spencer cohomology, the left hand side of this equation gives

[\sigma^{*}\circ(h_{*}{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\circ{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})]=\Theta^{(1)},

i.e., the torsion class of
M^{(1)}
. On the other hand, since
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{\sigma}
is torsionless, the right hand side of (
3.4
) gives the curvature class

[\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}_{\sigma}]=\Omega,

so the theorem follows.

It remains to prove that equation (
3.4
) holds. For that, we only need to observe that both sides act in the same way on
\Omega^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
, and that both derivations have the same symbol precisely because
h
is flat, i.e, because of (
3.3
).
∎

For PDEs, Goldschmidt formulated a criterion for when a PDE is formally integrable (
[
20
, Thm 8.1]
). The following result is an analogue (or rather, extension) of that result for relative algebroids.

Theorem 3.10

(Formal integrability criterion)
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid. Suppose that

(i)

(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is 1-integrable,

(ii)

H^{k,2}(\tau)=0
for all
k\geq 0
,

then
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is formally integrable.

For the proof we need the following two lemmas.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid with tableau map
\tau
. Suppose that
p_{1}\colon M^{(1)}\to M
is surjective and that
\tau^{(1)}\to M
has constant rank. Then
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is 1-integrable.

Proof.

Follows from Proposition 3.5 in
[
20
]
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\tau
be a tableau bundle such that
\tau^{(1)}
has constant rank and
H^{k,2}(\tau)=0
for all
k\geq 0
. Then
\tau^{(k)}
has constant rank for all
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma 1.5.6 in
[
33
]
holds in this setting.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

We use induction to show that the
k
-th prolongation of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
exists. By assumption, the 1st prolongation exists. So assume that
k\geq 1
and that we already know that the
k
-th prolongation exists. We claim that

(a)

\tau^{(k+1)}=(\tau^{(k)})^{(1)}
has constant rank, and

(b)

p_{k+1}\colon M^{(k+1)}=(M^{(k)})^{(1)}\to M^{(k)}
is surjective.

Then Lemma
3.11
shows that the
k
-th prolongation is 1-integrable, so we are done.

Item (a) follows immediately from Lemma
3.12
and the assumptions in the statement. To prove item (b), note that by Theorem
3.8
, under the map

H^{0,2}(\tau^{(k-1)})\hookrightarrow H^{-1,2}(\tau^{(k)}),

the torsion class of the
k
-th prolongation takes values in
H^{0,2}(\tau^{(k-1)})
. But,

H^{0,2}(\tau^{(k-1)})\simeq H^{k,2}(\tau),

which vanishes under our assumptions. The vanishing of this class implies that the projection
p_{k}
is surjective.
∎

Corollary 3.13
.

If
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
has an involutive tableau
\tau
and vanishing torsion class, then it is formally integrable.

Definition 3.14
.

A
relative Lie algebroid
is a formally integrable relative algebroid.

The appearance of the term “Lie” in the terminology is motivated by the full prolongation tower of a formally integrable relative algebroid:

\textstyle{{\left(B^{(\infty)},{\mathrm{D}}^{(\infty)}\right)\colon\ldots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{(k-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}^{(k)}}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}
\textstyle{M^{(\infty)}\colon\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{k}}
\textstyle{M^{(k-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{M}

In particular, although
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}
does not make sense,
the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}^{(\infty)}
, defined on profinite sections of
B^{(\infty)}\to M^{(\infty)}
(i.e., on those that locally factor through some
M^{(k)}
) squares to zero:

\left({\mathrm{D}}^{(\infty)}\right)^{2}=0,

so
(B^{(\infty)},{\mathrm{D}}^{(\infty)})
is a “profinite Lie algebroid of finite rank”. We refer to Section
6
for a concrete example of a profinite Lie algebroid arising in this way.

3.3.
Some examples

In this section, we discuss several simple examples that illustrate the various issues that can arise with prolongations of relative algebroids. We provide examples of relative algebroids that:
(i) are of infinite type,
(ii) are of finite type,
(iii) do not admit a prolongation, and
(iv) have a first prolongation but not a second prolongation.

While such examples already exist in the context of PDEs, the ones presented here are independent of PDE theory. These examples should also help the reader develop intuition for the general framework.

Example 3.15

(Relative vector fields and control systems)
.

If a relative algebroid is 1-integrable and has an involutive tableau with non-zero Cartan characters, it is always of infinite type. Such examples, with the smallest rank, arise from
relative vector fields
.

A
vector field relative to a submersion

p\colon M\to N
is a section
X\in\Gamma(p^{*}TN)
. It gives rise to a relative algebroid

\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{X}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{N}

where the derivation is given by

{\mathrm{D}}_{X}\colon C^{\infty}(N)\to\Omega^{1}(\underline{\mathbb{R}}),\quad{\mathrm{D}}_{X}(f)_{m}=X_{m}(f){\mathrm{d}}t,

where
{\mathrm{d}}t
is a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{*}
. Conversely, any relative algebroid with vector bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
gives rise to a relative vector field. In particular, the prolongation of a relative vector field is again a relative vector field.

For a relative vector field
X
, the tableau map of the corresponding algebroid is

\displaystyle\tau={\overline{\nabla}}X\colon

\displaystyle\ker{\mathrm{d}}p\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{R},p^{*}TN)\cong p^{*}TN,

\displaystyle\tau(v)(\lambda):

\displaystyle=\lambda\left.\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}X_{\gamma(t)}\right|_{t=0}

where
\gamma(t)
is a curve in a fiber of
p
with
\dot{\gamma}(0)=v
.
The Spencer differential
\delta_{\tau}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{R},\ker{\mathrm{d}}p)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}\mathbb{R},p^{*}TN)
vanishes identically, and therefore the tableau is involutive, with Cartan character

s_{1}=\operatorname{rank}(\ker{\mathrm{d}}p)=\dim M-\dim N.

A lift of
{\mathrm{D}}_{X}
to
\underline{\mathbb{R}}\to M
is a vector field
\tilde{X}
on
M
such that
{\mathrm{d}}p(\tilde{X})=X
. The corresponding derivation
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{X}
always squares to zero, so the relative algebroid has vanishing torsion and curvature, and is therefore formally integrable.

A local realization can be described explicitly as a curve
\gamma^{M}\colon I\to M
such that
\gamma^{N}:=p\circ\gamma^{M}
satisfies the ODE

(3.5)

\dot{\gamma}^{N}(t)=X_{\gamma^{M}(t)}.

In local coordinates, if
(x^{\mu},y^{\varrho})
are submersion coordinates on
M
, then the vector field looks like

X=X^{\mu}(x,y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}},

so the ODE (
3.5
) takes the form

(3.6)

\dot{x}^{\mu}(t)=X^{\mu}(x(t),y(t)).

This shows that locally, realizations (integral curves) are completely determined by the choice of the
\dim M-\dim N
family of functions
y^{\varrho}(t)
and the choice of an initial point
(x_{0},y_{0})
.
Equation (
3.6
) is a control system (with unspecified observables), where the choice of the functions
y^{\varrho}(t)

is
the control input. In particular, according to the results in
[
23
]
, when the vector field is real analytic, then
N
is partitioned into a singular foliation with the property that for each point
n_{0}\in N
, the integral curves of
X
through
n_{0}
saturate a neighborhood of
n_{0}
inside the leaf through
n_{0}
.

As a very particular case, consider
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to*
with the zero relative vector field. A realization of the corresponding relative algebroid is just an
\mathbb{R}
-valued function
x^{1}(t)
defined on an interval. The prolongation tower consists of a sequence of vector fields
X_{k}
relative to the projection
p_{k}\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
,

\textstyle{{\left(\underline{\mathbb{R}},{\mathrm{D}}_{X_{\infty}}\right)\colon\ldots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{X_{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{X_{0}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\colon\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{k}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{*}

Denoting by
(x^{1},\dots,x^{k})
the coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, the relative vector fields
X_{k}
are given by

X_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{i+1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\quad X_{\infty}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x^{i+1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}.

The profinite vector field
X_{\infty}
appears later in Section
6
.

Example 3.16

(Finite-type relative algebroid)
.

Consider the submersion
p\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},(x,y,z)\mapsto(x,y)
and the relative algebroid
(\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{2},p,{\mathrm{D}})
determined by

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{1}=\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{2}=0,\\
{\mathrm{D}}x=z\theta^{1},\\
{\mathrm{D}}y=z\theta^{2}.\end{cases}

To compute the prolongation, we find an extension
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
of
{\mathrm{D}}
satisfying

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}({\mathrm{D}}x)=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}z\wedge\theta^{1}+z\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}({\mathrm{D}}y)=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}z\wedge\theta^{2}.

Writing
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}z=\alpha\theta^{1}+\beta\theta^{2}
, we see that we must have

\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}z=z\theta^{2}.

This defines a prolongation where
M^{(1)}\cong M=\mathbb{R}^{3}
, so
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
is a derivation relative to the identity. At this point, we only know that
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\circ{\mathrm{D}}=0
, but one checks easily that
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}=0
. Hence, the first prolongation is actually a Lie algebroid and so
M^{(k)}\simeq M^{(1)}
for all
k\geq 1
. The resulting algebroid
(\underline{\mathbb{R}^{2}},\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}})\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is isomorphic to an action algebroid
\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is the non-abelian two-dimensional Lie algebra.

Example 3.17

(1-Integrable relative algebroid that is not 2-integrable)
.

Let us modify the derivation in the previous example by setting

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{1}=\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{2}=0,\\
{\mathrm{D}}x=z\theta^{1},\\
{\mathrm{D}}y=z\theta^{2}-\theta^{1}.\end{cases}

Proceeding as before, the first prolongation
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
is now given by

\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}z=\theta^{1}+z\theta^{2}.

However, in this case
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}^{2}z=2\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}\neq 0
, so the second prolongation does not exist.

Example 3.18

(Torsion from tableau of derivations)
.

In the previous example, the equations determining the prolongation and obstructions to integrability arose solely from the symbol tableau of the relative algebroid. We now illustrate how the full tableau of derivations can give rise to torsion. Of course, this can only happen when the rank is at least three, otherwise the symbol torsion completely determines the torsion.
One way to achieve this is to consider relative algebroids with zero anchor. So, consider the relative algebroid
(\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3},p,{\mathrm{D}})
, with
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to*
and
{\mathrm{D}}
determined by

(3.7)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{1}=x\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{2}=x\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3},\\
{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{3}=x\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{1},\end{cases}

where
x
is the coordinate on
\mathbb{R}
. A lift
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
of
{\mathrm{D}}
is determined by
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}x=x_{1}\theta^{1}+x_{2}\theta^{2}+x_{3}\theta^{3}
and the torsion is the 2-derivation
(\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\circ{\mathrm{D}})
relative to
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\to*
defined by

\begin{cases}\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\circ{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{1}=(x_{3}-x^{2})\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3},\\
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\circ{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{2}=(x_{1}-x^{2})\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3},\\
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}\circ{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{3}=(x_{2}-x^{2})\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3}.\end{cases}

We find that the first prolongation space is

M^{(1)}=\{x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{3}=x^{2}\},

so the first prolongation is given by

{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}x=x^{2}(\theta^{1}+\theta^{2}+\theta^{3}).

Note that the curvature is only zero at
x=0
:

\left({\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\right)^{2}x=x^{3}(\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}+\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3}+\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{1}).

Hence, to obtain an actual Lie algebroid one has to restrict to the space
x=0
, in which case the relative algebroid is just the abelian Lie algebra
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

3.4.
Realizations and prolongations

Prolongations arose as a tool for constructing and computing obstructions to the existence of realizations. The realizations of the prolongations are related to realizations of the original relative algebroid in the following manner.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid. Then:

(i)

If
(B_{1},p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
is a prolongation, then any realization of
(B_{1},p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
induces a realization of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.

(ii)

If
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is 1-integrable, then realizations of the canonical prolongation
(B^{(1)},p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
are in 1-1 correspondence with realizations of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.

Proof.

To prove (i), let
(B_{1},p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
be a prolongation and let
(P,r_{1},\theta_{1})
be a realization of
(B_{1},p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
. The fact that
(P,p_{1}\circ r_{1},(p_{1})_{*}\circ\theta_{1})
is a realization of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
follows by restricting to
\Omega^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
the identity valid on
\Omega^{1}_{B}
:

{\mathrm{d}}\circ\left(\theta_{1}\right)^{*}=\left(\theta_{1}\right)^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{1}.

To prove (ii), let
(P,r,\theta)
be a realization of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
. By Proposition
2.12
, we can define the map

r^{(1)}\colon P\to M^{(1)},\quad r^{(1)}(p)=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}_{\theta_{p}}:=\left(\theta^{-1}_{p}\right)^{*}\circ\left({\mathrm{d}}\theta^{*}\big|_{p}\right).

Clearly,
p_{1}\circ r^{(1)}=r
, and since
B^{(1)}=p_{1}^{*}B
there is a natural bundle map

(\theta^{(1)},r^{(1)})\colon TP\to B^{(1)},\text{ with }(p_{1})_{*}\circ\theta^{(1)}=\theta.

Using that
\theta
is a realization, we find that

{\mathrm{d}}\circ\left(\theta^{(1)}\right)^{*}\circ p_{1}^{*}={\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}=\theta^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}=\left(\theta^{(1)}\right)^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\circ p_{1}^{*},\mbox{ on $\Omega^{\bullet}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}$.}

From our formula for
r^{(1)}
and the tautological nature of
{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}
it also follows that

{\mathrm{d}}\circ\left(r^{(1)}\right)^{*}(f)=\left(\theta^{(1)}\right)^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}(f),\mbox{ for $f\in C^{\infty}(M)$,}

so
(r^{(1)},\theta^{(1)})
is a realization of
B^{(1)}
. The construction in (i) when applied to the 1st prolongation is an inverse to this construction, so we have a 1:1 correspondence.
∎

In the analytic setting, the existence of realizations follows from the classical Cartan–Kähler theory; see the discussion in Section 3 of Bryant’s unpublished lecture notes
[
8
]
.

Theorem 3.20
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},\mathcal{D})
be an analytic relative Lie algebroid. Then, for each
k
and each
x\in M^{(k)}
, there exists a realization through
x
.

Proof.

Since, by assumption,
(B,\overline{\nabla},\mathcal{D})
is formally integrable, induction and Proposition
3.19
show that it is enough to prove the result for
k=1
. Moreover, since every tableau is involutive after finitely many prolongations, we can assume that the tableau
\tau
is involutive. But then, the realization problem for
B^{(1)}
in local analytic coordinates (see Remark
2.8
and Example
3.5
) satisfies the assumptions of
[
8
, Thm. 3]
. The latter result shows that there exists a realization through every point in
M^{(1)}
.
∎

3.5.
Naturality

Let
(\varphi,p)\colon(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1})\to(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2})
be a morphism of flat foliated vector bundles covering a map
p\colon(M_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to(M_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})
, such that
\varphi
is a fiberwise isomorphism. As we saw in Section
1.5
,
\varphi
induces a bundle map

\varphi_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1})}\to\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2})}.

More generally, in this situation, there is a bundle map

\varphi_{*}\colon\operatorname{Hom}\left(\wedge^{l}B_{1},\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1})}\right)\to\operatorname{Hom}\left(\wedge^{l}B_{2},\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2})}\right)

that intertwines the Spencer differentials:
\delta\circ\varphi_{*}=\varphi_{*}\circ\delta
. All constructions with derivations that we discussed before behave naturally relative to these induced maps.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
(\varphi,p)\colon(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})\to(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2},{\mathrm{D}}_{2})
be a morphism of relative algebroids, which is a fiberwise isomorphism. Then

(i)

The tableau maps are
(\varphi_{*},p_{*})
-related:
\varphi_{*}\circ\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}\circ p_{*}
. In particular,
\varphi
induces a morphism in Spencer cohomology

\varphi_{*}\colon H^{k,l}(\tau_{1})\to H^{k,l}(\tau_{2}),

that maps the torsion class of
B_{1}
to the one of
B_{2}
;

(ii)

If
(P,r,\theta)
is a realization of
(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
, then
(P,p\circ r,\varphi\circ\theta)
is a realization of
(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2},{\mathrm{D}}_{2})
;

(iii)

If
(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
and
(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2},{\mathrm{D}}_{2})
are
k
-integrable, then
\varphi
induces a morphism of relative algebroids
(\varphi^{(k)},p^{(k)})\colon(B_{1}^{(k)},(p_{1})_{k},{\mathrm{D}}^{(k)}_{1})\to(B_{2}^{(k)},(p_{2})_{k},{\mathrm{D}}^{(k)}_{2})
that is fiberwise an isomorphism;

(iv)

If
(B_{1},{\overline{\nabla}}^{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
and
(B_{2},{\overline{\nabla}}^{2},{\mathrm{D}}_{2})
are formally integrable, then
\varphi
induces a morphism
