63.85968pt\raise-6.12498pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pr}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.17711pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.17711pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proposition 4.3
.

Every relative algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
is canonically isomorphic to the pullback of the universal relative Lie algebroid
(A,p_{1},\breve{\mathrm{D}})
along its classifying map.

Proof.

Note that
p_{1}\circ c_{D}=p
, and that the pullback
c_{D}^{*}\breve{\mathrm{D}}
coincides with
{\mathrm{D}}
under the canonical identification
c^{*}_{D}p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}\cong p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
.
∎

Example 4.4
.

When
N=\{*\}
, so
A=V
is a vector space, we have

\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V}\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}V,V)\to*.

A relative algebroid
(V,p,{\mathrm{D}})
, for
p\colon M\to\{*\}
, is the same thing as a skew-symmetric bilinear map
[\cdot,\cdot]\colon V\times V\to C^{\infty}(M,V)
, viewed as a bracket on
V
relative to
p
. This is determined by a map
c_{{\mathrm{D}}}\colon M\to\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}V,V)
,
c_{{\mathrm{D}}}(x)(v,w)=[v,w](x)
, which is precisely the classifying map. This construction was already considered by Bryant in relation to his Theorem 4 in
[
8
]
.

Example 4.5
.

For almost Lie algebroids, the previous proposition says that any such structure
{\mathrm{D}}
on a fixed vector bundle
p\colon A\to N
is obtained by pulling back the tautological derivation
\breve{\mathrm{D}}
along a section
c_{{\mathrm{D}}}\colon N\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
:
c_{{\mathrm{D}}}^{*}\breve{\mathrm{D}}={\mathrm{D}}
.

Example 4.6
.

The classifying map of the first prolongation of a 1-integrable relative algebroid
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
is the inclusion
M^{(1)}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B}
. In fact, given an algebroid
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
relative to a submersion
p_{1}\colon M_{1}\to M
with classifying map
c_{{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}\colon M_{1}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B}
, one has that:

(i)

(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
is an
extension
of
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
if and only if
c_{{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}
takes values in the space of pointwise lifts
L\subset\mathcal{D}^{1}_{B}
;

(ii)

(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
is a
prolongation
of
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
if and only if the image of
c_{{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}
is contained in
M^{(1)}
.

In particular, among all prolongations of
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
, the canonical prolongation is the universal one.

Next, we will study properties of the universal relative Lie algebroid. We start by justifying the use of the term “Lie”.

Proposition 4.7
.

The universal relative Lie algebroid of a vector bundle
A\to N
is formally integrable.

Proof.

The tableau of the universal algebroid is the identity map
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
, so it is involutive by Proposition
1.51
. The torsion class vanishes automatically because
H^{-1,2}(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A})=0
by Lemma
1.52
. Then, by Corollary
3.13
of Goldschmidt’s Formal Integrability Criterion, the universal algebroid is formally integrable.
∎

The realizations of the universal relative Lie algebroid have a nice geometric interpretation. For that,
given a manifold
P
and a vector bundle
A\to N
, by an
A
-coframe
on
P
we mean a bundle map
\theta_{A}\colon TP\to A
covering a map
r_{N}\colon P\to N
which is fiberwise an isomorphism. These objects have appeared in the literature under the name “generalized coframes” in the context of Dirac spinors coupled to Einstein’s equations – see
[
29
]
.

Proposition 4.8
.

The realizations of the universal relative Lie algebroid of
A\to N
are in one-to-one correspondence with manifolds equipped with
A
-coframes.

Proof.

In one direction, it is clear that a realization
(\theta,r)\colon TP\to p_{1}^{*}A
of the universal relative Lie algebroid
(A,p,\breve{\mathrm{D}})
, gives rise to the
A
-frame
\theta_{A}\colon TP\to A
covering
r_{N}:=p_{1}\circ r
given by

\theta_{A}=\pr_{A}\circ\,\theta.

In the opposite direction, given an
A
-coframe
\theta_{A}\colon TP\to A
covering a map
r_{N}\colon P\to N
, we can define a bundle map
(\theta,r)\colon TP\to p_{1}^{*}A
which is a fiberwise isomorphism by

\begin{cases}r(p):={\mathrm{D}}_{r_{N}(p)},\\
\theta(v_{p}):=({\mathrm{D}}_{r_{N}(p)},\theta_{A}(v_{p})),\end{cases}\quad(v_{p}\in T_{p}P),

where
{\mathrm{D}}_{r_{N}(p)}\in(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A})_{r_{N}(p)}
is given by

{\mathrm{D}}_{r_{N}(p)}\alpha:=(\theta_{A})_{*}\circ{\mathrm{d}}_{p}\circ\theta_{A}^{*}(\alpha),\quad(\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(A)).

This ensures that the condition

\theta^{*}\circ\breve{\mathrm{D}}={\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}\circ p_{1}^{*}

holds, so
(\theta,r)
is a realization of
(A,p_{1},\breve{\mathrm{D}}).

These constructions are inverse to each other, so the proposition follows.
∎

Recall that a relative algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
is called standard when its tableau map
\tau\colon\ker{\mathrm{d}}p\to p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
is fiberwise injective.

Proposition 4.9
.

An algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
relative to a submersion
p\colon M\to N
is standard if and only if its classifying map
c_{{\mathrm{D}}}\colon M\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
is an immersion.

Proof.

Note that
p=p_{1}\circ c_{{\mathrm{D}}}
. This means that the classifying map
c_{{\mathrm{D}}}\colon M\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
is an immersion if and only if its vertical derivative
{\mathrm{d}}c_{{\mathrm{D}}}\big|_{\ker{\mathrm{d}}p}\colon\ker{\mathrm{d}}p\to\ker{\mathrm{d}}p_{1}
is fiberwise injective. We claim that the tableau map
\tau\colon\ker{\mathrm{d}}p\to p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
is given by

(4.1)

\tau(X_{m})=(m,{\mathrm{d}}c_{{\mathrm{D}}}(X_{m})),\quad(X_{m}\in\ker{\mathrm{d}}_{m}p),

so the result follows. To prove the above formula, just observe that we have, by Proposition
4.3
,

\tau(X)_{m}={\overline{\nabla}}_{X_{m}}{\mathrm{D}}={\overline{\nabla}}_{X_{m}}(c_{{\mathrm{D}}}^{*}\breve{\mathrm{D}})={\overline{\nabla}}_{{\mathrm{d}}c_{{\mathrm{D}}}(X_{m})}\breve{\mathrm{D}}={\mathrm{d}}c_{\mathrm{D}}(X_{m}).\qed

4.2.
Restriction

Almost relative algebroids are so flexible that they can easily be restricted to subspaces. From the point of view of Cartan’s realization problem, imposing restrictions on a relative algebroid structure is equivalent to adding extra conditions to the realization problem of the original relative algebroid. This will be clear from examples at the end of this section. For now, let us be precise about what we mean by “restriction”.

In this section we fix an ambient relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.

Definition 4.10
.

A map
q\colon Q\to M
is
invariant
for
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
if the image of
{\mathrm{d}}q
contains the image of the anchor of
{\mathrm{D}}
, i.e., if for every
x\in Q
,

\sigma({\mathrm{D}}_{q(x)})\in\operatorname{Hom}(B_{q(x)},\operatorname{im}({\mathrm{d}}_{x}q)/\mathcal{F}_{q(x)})\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}(B_{q(x)},\nu(\mathcal{F}_{q(x)})).

Remark 4.11
.

In the special case of an algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
relative to a submersion
p\colon M\to N
, the invariance condition on a map
q\colon Q\to M
says that

\operatorname{im}\rho_{p(x)}\subseteq\operatorname{im}T_{x}(p\circ q),\quad\mbox{ for all $x\in Q$.}

If
(A,{\mathrm{D}})
is an almost Lie algebroid and
q
is an immersion, we recover the usual notion of invariant submanifold for such an algebroid.

Proposition 4.12
.

Suppose that a map
q\colon Q\to M
satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

q
is invariant for
{\mathrm{D}}
;

(b)

({\mathrm{d}}_{x}q)^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{q(x)})
has constant rank for all
x\in Q
.

Then there exists a unique relative algebroid
(q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{Q})
for which the map
q_{*}\colon q^{*}B\to B
is a morphism of relative algebroids.

Remark 4.13
.

The morphism
q_{*}\colon(q^{*}B,q^{*}{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}}_{Q})\to(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
is a fiberwise isomorphism, so it follows from Proposition
3.21
that:

(i)

realizations of
(q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{Q})
yield realizations of
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
;

(ii)

there is an induced morphism at level of the Spencer cohomologies of the tableaux
q_{*}\colon H^{k,l}(\tau_{Q})\to H^{k,l}(\tau)
, which relates the torsion classes; however, this map is neither injective nor surjective in general;

(iii)

if both algebroids are
k
-integrable, there is an induced algebroid morphism
q_{*}\colon((q^{*}B)^{(k)},(p_{Q})_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{Q}^{(k)})\to(B^{(k)},p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}^{(k)})
;

(iv)

if both algebroids are formally integrable, there is an induced algebroid morphism
q_{*}\colon((q^{*}B)^{\infty},{\mathrm{D}}_{Q}^{\infty})\to(B^{\infty},{\mathrm{D}}^{\infty})
.

Note also that if
q\colon Q\to M
is
transverse
to
\mathcal{F}
, then both conditions (a) and (b) in the proposition are automatically satisfied.

Proof.

The regularity condition (b) means that the pullback foliation
q^{!}\mathcal{F}
exists. It follows that one has a flat foliated vector bundle
(q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla})
over
(Q,q^{!}\mathcal{F})
, as well as a map
of foliated vector bundles

q_{*}:=(q,\pr)\colon(q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla})\to(B,{\overline{\nabla}}).

Since this is a fiberwise isomorphism, as we saw in Section
1.5
, it induces a bundle map

q_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla})}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}

which covers
q
.

It is easy to check that the short exact sequence in Lemma
1.25
is natural in
q_{*}
. This means that there is a commutative diagram of short exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}q^{*}B,q^{*}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(q^{*}B,\nu(q^{!}\mathcal{F}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{q^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}B,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{q^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{q^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(B,\nu(\mathcal{F}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Both vertical arrows on the sides are injective, and therefore so is the middle vertical arrow. Since
q\colon Q\to M
is an invariant map, the section
q^{*}{\mathrm{D}}
is in the image of
q_{*}
, so there is a unique relative derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{Q}
on
(Q,q^{!}\mathcal{F},q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla})
that satisfies
q_{*}({\mathrm{D}}_{Q})=q^{*}{\mathrm{D}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Definition 4.14
.

If
q
is an injective immersion that satisfies the conditions of Proposition
4.12
, we call
(q^{*}B,q^{*}\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{Q})
the
restriction
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
to
Q
.

Example 4.15
.

If
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
is a 1-integrable relative algebroid, then its first prolongation
(B,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
is the restriction of the tautological relative algebroid
(A,p_{1},\breve{\mathrm{D}})
to
M^{(1)}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{1}_{A}
.

It should be noted that, in general, restriction does not preserve any kind of integrability of the relative algebroid. From the point of view of the realization problem, restriction amounts to adding extra equations to the problem. So a problem that originally had realizations may stop having them.

The vanishing locus of the anchor is always invariant.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid and let
Q\subset M
be a submanifold along which
\rho
vanishes. Then
Q
is an invariant submanifold.

Proof.

In this case,
\operatorname{im}\rho_{x}=0
for all
x\in Q
, so
Q
is clearly invariant.
∎

If
(B^{(\infty)},{\mathrm{D}}^{(\infty)})
is the prolongation tower of a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
, then the kernel of the anchor
\ker\rho^{(\infty)}_{x_{0}}
at each point
x_{0}\in M^{(\infty)}
is a Lie algebra. This is the Lie algebra of a group of symmetries of a realization whose image contains
p_{\infty}(x_{0})\in M^{(\infty)}
. So, in general, the larger the group of symmetries of a realization of a geometric problem is, the larger the kernel of the anchor of the corresponding relative algebroid must be.

Example 4.17

(Submanifolds where anchor has constant rank)
.

In general, submanifolds along which the rank of the anchor is constant are not invariant, unless involutivity conditions are imposed.

For example, take the algebroid
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
(relative to the identity), with derivation defined by

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{1}={\mathrm{D}}\theta^{2}=0,\\
{\mathrm{D}}x=-y\theta^{1},\\
{\mathrm{D}}y=\theta^{2}.\end{cases}

The corresponding anchor satisfies
\rho(e_{1})=-y\partial_{x}
,
\rho(e_{2})=\partial_{y}
. Therefore, the submanifold
Q=\{\operatorname{rank}\rho=1\}=\{y=0\}
is not invariant. In this example, there are no realizations anywhere, as
{\mathrm{D}}^{2}x=\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}\neq 0
.

Example 4.18

(Universal bundle of Lie algebras)
.

The universal bundle of Lie algebra structures on a vector space
V
can be constructed, in an
ad hoc
manner, as the bundle of Lie algebras – a Lie algebroid with zero anchor –
\underline{V}\to\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
where

\hat{\mathfrak{g}}=\{c\in\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}V,V)\ |\ \operatorname{Jac}_{c}=0\}

with
\operatorname{Jac}_{c}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})=c(v_{1},c(v_{2},v_{3}))+\mathrm{c.p.}
for
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in V
. The bracket on
\underline{V}\to\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
is the tautological bracket:
[v_{1},v_{2}](c)=c(v_{1},v_{2})
. A realization of this algebroid above a point
c\in\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
is a local Lie group integrating the Lie algebra
(V,c)
.

This Lie algebroid arises by imposing maximal symmetry on the classifying algebroid for all
V
-coframes. Namely, let
p_{1}\colon\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V}\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}V,V)\to*
be the tautological relative algebroid corresponding to a vector space
V
. Consider its prolongation
(p_{1}^{*}V,p_{2},{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)})
, relative to the projection
p_{2}\colon(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V})^{(1)}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V}
, where

(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V})^{(1)}=\big\{\xi_{c}\in p_{1}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(V,\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}V,V))\ |\ \operatorname{Jac}_{c}=\delta\xi_{c}\big\},\quad p_{2}(\xi_{c})=c.

The anchor of this prolongation at
\xi_{c}\in(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V})^{(1)}
vanishes if and only if
\xi_{c}=0
. The restriction of
p_{2}
to this invariant subvariety gives a canonical identification
\{\rho^{(1)}=0\}\cong\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
. The restricted relative algebroid is precisely
\underline{V}\to\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
, the universal bundle of Lie algebra structures on
V
.

Realizations of the relative algebroid
\underline{V}\to\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
correspond to
V
-coframes with maximal symmetry: these are just local Lie groups with their Maurer-Cartan forms!

Example 4.19

(Jacobi manifolds)
.

Fixing a vector space
V
, Bryant in
[
8
]
defines a
Jacobi manifold
as a submanifold
M\subset\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V}\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}V,V)
such that

\operatorname{Jac}_{c}\in\delta(\operatorname{Hom}(\underline{V},T_{c}M)),\quad\mbox{for all $c\in M$},

where
\delta\colon\operatorname{Hom}(V,\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V})\to\mathcal{D}^{2}_{V}
is the Spencer differential. In our language, this is the same as saying that the restriction of the tautological algebroid to
M
has vanishing torsion class. Theorem 4 in
[
8
]
states that, when
M
is real analytic, if
T_{c}M\subset\mathcal{D}^{1}_{V}
is an involutive tableau of derivations, then there exists a realization of the restricted algebroid through every point in
M
.

Example 4.20

(Riemannian metrics)
.

As we recalled in the introductory section, the orthonormal frame bundle of a Riemannian manifold has a canonical coframe
(\theta,\omega)
with values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{o}(n)
satisfying Cartan’s structure equations (
0.1
). This suggests that the realization problem for (locally orthonormal frame bundles of) Riemannian metrics can be obtained by restricting
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{o}(n)}
to derivations of the form

(4.2)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta=-\omega\wedge\theta,\\
{\mathrm{D}}\omega=R(\theta\wedge\theta)-\omega\wedge\omega\end{cases}

The space of such derivations is an affine subspace of
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{o}(n)}
parametrized by
R\in\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathfrak{o}(n))
.

The resulting restricted algebroid has a non-zero torsion class, and therefore the algebroid needs to be restricted further to the subspace where the torsion class vanishes. The torsion class can be computed by taking an extension
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
of
{\mathrm{D}}
. Such extension is completely determined by
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}R
, and to find this value one applies
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
to (
4.2
):

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\mathrm{D}}^{2}\theta=-{\mathrm{D}}\omega\wedge\theta+\omega\wedge{\mathrm{D}}\theta=R(\theta\wedge\theta)\wedge\theta

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathrm{D}}\omega=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}R\wedge(\theta\wedge\theta)+R({\mathrm{D}}\theta\wedge\theta)-R(\theta\wedge{\mathrm{D}}\theta)-{\mathrm{D}}\omega\wedge\omega+\omega\wedge{\mathrm{D}}\omega

\displaystyle=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}R\wedge(\theta\wedge\theta)

The torsion class can be identified with
R(\theta\wedge\theta)\wedge\theta
, and its vanishing is precisely the first (algebraic) Bianchi identity.

One may also wonder about the second Bianchi identity. This will be a consequence of the vanishing of the curvature class, when imposing “
\mathfrak{o}(n)
-invariance of the prolongation”. In order to implement this one needs to include in the notion of a relative algebroid a structure group. Such a concept, which may be called a
relative
G
-structure algebroid
, would extend the notion of
G
-structure Lie algebroid studied in
[
19
,
18
]
(see also Section
7
).

4.3.
Systatic foliation and reduction

Recall that a relative algebroid is
standard
when its tableau map
\tau
is fiberwise injective. As we saw in the previous section, this happens precisely when the classifying map is an immersion. We will now show that the directions in which the tableau map is zero – i.e., the directions in which the classifying map is constant – are essentially “redundant” from the perspective of the realization problem. The notion of systatic space and inessential invariants goes back to Cartan – a modern formulation and discussion can be found in
[
16
]
.

Again, in this section we fix a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
over
(M,\mathcal{F})
. We also assume that the kernel of its tableau map
\tau\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}
has constant rank.

Definition 4.21
.

The
systatic foliation
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is the foliation

\mathcal{F}_{\text{sys}}:=\ker\tau\subset\mathcal{F},

where
\tau
is the tableau map of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
.

Note that
\mathcal{F}_{\text{sys}}
is involutive because the
\mathcal{F}
-connection
\overline{\nabla}
induced on
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}
is flat. Next, we present two useful characterizations of the systatic foliation.

Proposition 4.22
.

For a relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
the systatic foliation is given by

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{sys}}=\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\overline{\nabla}_{X}[b_{1},b_{2}]=0,\mbox{ for all $b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}$ }\},

where
[\cdot,\cdot]
denotes the bracket associated to
{\mathrm{D}}
.

Proof.

By (
2.4
), for each
X\in\mathcal{F}
, we have

{\overline{\nabla}}_{X}{\mathrm{D}}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\overline{\nabla}_{X}[b_{1},b_{2}]=0,\text{ for all }b_{1},b_{2}\in\Gamma_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}.\qed

Proposition 4.23
.

The systatic foliation of an algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
relative to a submersion coincides with the connected components of the fibers of the classifying map
c_{{\mathrm{D}}}
.

Proof.

By (
4.1
), we have
\ker\tau=\ker{\mathrm{d}}c_{{\mathrm{D}}}
,
so the result follows.
∎

Let us now discuss how to get rid of directions along the systatic foliation.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid.
A foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{sys}}
is called
inessential
if
it is a simple foliation and
\overline{\nabla}
has no holonomy along its leaves.

Notice that an inessential foliation of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is given by the fibers of a submersion
q\colon M\to M_{\mathrm{red}}
. The foliation
\mathcal{F}
descends to a foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{red}}
of the leaf space
M_{\mathrm{red}}
characterized by

\mathcal{F}=({\mathrm{d}}q)^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{red}}).

Moreover, by Proposition
1.17
, there is a unique flat foliated bundle
(B_{\mathrm{red}},{\overline{\nabla}})
over
M_{\mathrm{red}}
whose pullback under
q\colon M\to M_{\mathrm{red}}
is
(B,{\overline{\nabla}})
.

We use these notations in the statement of the following theorem showing that the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
can also be reduced, preserving all its essential properties.

Theorem 4.25

(Reduction)
.

Let
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
be a relative algebroid and let
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{sys}}
be an inessential foliation with leaf space
q\colon M\to M_{\mathrm{red}}
. Then:

(i)

There exists a unique structure of a relative algebroid
(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
such that
q_{*}\colon B\to B_{\mathrm{red}}
is a morphism of relative algebroids;

(ii)

The tableaux
\tau
and
\tau_{\mathrm{red}}
of
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
and
(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
, have naturally isomorphic
Spencer cohomologies;

(iii)

The relative algebroid
(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})
is
k
-integrable if and only if
(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
is
k
-integrable;

(iv)

There is a one-to-one correspondence

\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{realizations $(P,r,\theta)$}\\
\\
\mbox{of $(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})$}\end{subarray}\right\}\overset{1:1}{\longleftrightarrow}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{realizations $(P,\tilde{r},\tilde{\theta})$ of $(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})$}\\
\mbox{together with a lift $r$ of $\tilde{r}$:}\\
\mbox{ $\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.11113pt\hbox{\kern 6.08385pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.11113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 33.74344pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.11113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.93164pt\raise-10.3361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.57848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.93164pt\raise-15.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.08385pt\raise-20.67221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.11113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.18129pt\raise-6.28125pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.05486pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.74344pt\raise-3.6292pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.68803pt\raise-27.28333pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.61111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{r}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.08385pt\raise-20.67221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.08385pt\raise-20.67221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.11113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M_{\mathrm{red}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces$
}\end{subarray}\right\}.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{sys}}
then the reduced algebroid
(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
is standard.

Remark 4.26
.

The reduced algebroid
(B_{\mathrm{red}},{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
can be seen as a
quotient
of the relative algebroid
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
by the pseudogroup generated by the flows of vector fields tangent to the inessential foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.

Remark 4.27
.

The prolongation of the reduction cannot be a (systatic) reduction of the prolongation. The reason is that the prolongation is always standard (see Lemma
3.6
). Intuitively, the prolongation before reduction adds extra equations that (locally) encode a map from a realization to the fibers of
q
, while this is lost in the prolongation of the reduction. However, since the morphism
q_{*}\colon(B,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}})\to(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
is a fiberwise isomorphism, it follows from Proposition
3.21
that, assuming
k
-integrability, there is an induced morphism between the
k
-prolongations.

Proof.

To prove item (i), note that the map of flat foliated vector bundles

q_{*}\colon(B,\overline{\nabla})\to(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla})

is a fiberwise isomorphism. It follows from Section
1.5
that we have a bundle map

q_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\to\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla})}.

We claim that there is a unique
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}}
which is
(q,q_{*})
-related to
{\mathrm{D}}
, so (i) holds.

To prove this claim, note that we have a commutative diagram of short exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}B,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}B,\nu(\mathcal{F}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k+1}B_{\mathrm{red}},B_{\mathrm{red}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{k}B_{\mathrm{red}},\nu(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{red}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Since the sides are fiberwise isomorphisms, so is the map in the middle. Now, because
\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)\subseteq\ker\tau
, we have that
{\overline{\nabla}}_{X}{\mathrm{D}}=0
for any
X\in\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)
, and since the holonomy of
{\overline{\nabla}}
vanishes along the directions of
\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)
, we conclude that the section
{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})})
is the pullback of a section
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B_{\mathrm{red}},{\overline{\nabla}})})
. This also means that
{\mathrm{D}}
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}}
are
(q,q_{*})
-related, so the claim follows.

In order to prove item (ii), we look at the relationship between the tableau maps of
B
and
B_{\mathrm{red}}
. According to Proposition
3.21
, the map
q_{*}
intertwines the tableau maps and the Spencer differentials. For
k\geq 1
this amounts to the commutativity of the following diagram where the rows are short exact sequences:

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}B,\operatorname{Hom}(S^{k}B,\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}B,\tau^{(k)})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{{q^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}B_{\mathrm{red}},\tau^{(k)}_{\mathrm{red}})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l+1}B,\operatorname{Hom}(S^{k-1}B,\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l+1}B,\tau^{(k-1)})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{{q^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l+1}B_{\mathrm{red}},\tau^{(k-1)}_{\mathrm{red}})}}

while for
k=0
one has the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}B,\ker({\mathrm{d}}q))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}B,\mathcal{F})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\tau}}
\textstyle{{q^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{l}B_{\mathrm{red}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{red}})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\tau_{\mathrm{red}}}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{D}^{l+1}_{(B,{\overline{\nabla}})}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}
\textstyle{{q^{*}\mathcal{D}^{l+1}_{(B_{\mathrm{red}},{\overline{\nabla}})}}}

Setting
l=1
, we conclude that
\tau^{(k)}
has constant rank if and only if
\tau_{\mathrm{red}}^{(k)}
has constant rank. Moreover, since the restriction

\tau\big|_{\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)}

is the zero tableau map, which is involutive, the map
q_{*}
descends to an isomorphism in cohomology:

q_{*}\colon H^{k,l}(\tau)\xrightarrow{\sim}q^{*}H^{k,l}(\tau_{\mathrm{red}}).

Now, using this isomorphism, item (iii) also follows: by Proposition
3.21
(i), torsion classes of the
k
-th prolongations are
q_{*}
-related; hence, if
B
and
B_{\mathrm{red}}
are
(k-1)
-integrable, then
B
is
k
-integrable if and only if
B_{\mathrm{red}}
is
k
-integrable.

Finally, to prove item (iv), observe that by Proposition
3.21
(ii) every realization
(P,r,\theta)
of
(B,{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}})
induces a realization
(P,\tilde{r},\tilde{\theta})
of
(B_{\mathrm{red}},{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
with
\tilde{r}=q\circ r
. Conversely,
given a realization
(P,\tilde{r},\tilde{\theta})
of
(B_{\mathrm{red}},{\overline{\nabla}},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}})
and a lift
r\colon P\to M
of
\tilde{r}
, using the fact that
q_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B,\overline{\nabla})}\to q^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{(B_{\mathrm{red}},\overline{\nabla})}
is an isomorphism of vector bundles, there is a unique vector bundle map
(\theta,r)\colon TP\to B
such that
\tilde{\theta}=q_{*}\circ\theta
. Moreover, since
q_{*}
is a morphism of relative algebroids, we have

\displaystyle q_{*}({\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}-\theta^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}\circ q^{*}-\theta^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}\circ q^{*}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{d}}\circ(q_{*}\circ\theta)^{*}-(q_{*}\circ\theta)^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}}={\mathrm{d}}\circ\tilde{\theta}^{*}-\tilde{\theta}^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathrm{red}}=0.

Since
q_{*}
is a fiberwise isomorphism we must have
{\mathrm{d}}\circ\theta^{*}=\theta^{*}\circ{\mathrm{D}}
, so
(\theta,r)
is a morphism of almost relative algebroids.

These constructions are inverse to each other, so this completes the proof.
∎

5.
Relative connections and PDEs

We will now show that any partial differential equation can be recast as a relative algebroid in such a way that the formal theory of prolongations
[
20
]
coincides with the prolongation theory for relative algebroids. The relative algebroid of a PDE arises from a relative connection, so we start by discussing this notion.

5.1.
Relative connections and the Cartan distribution

Let
N\xrightarrow{q}X
be a submersion. An Ehresmann connection for
q
is a splitting
TN\cong\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)\oplus q^{*}TX
. Sometimes, however, the splitting does not arise on the level of
N
, but rather depends on additional coordinates. Let us illustrate this with an important example.

Example 5.1
.

Let
q\colon N\to X
be any submersion. The bundle
p_{1}\colon J^{1}q\to N
of first jets of local sections can be identified with the bundle of horizontal complements of
\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)
in
TN
, that is

(J^{1}q)_{n}\cong\{C_{n}\subset T_{n}N:C_{n}\oplus\ker({\mathrm{d}}_{n}q)=T_{n}N\}.

There is no canonical splitting of
TN
into the components
\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)
and
q^{*}TX
, but there is a tautological splitting of
p_{1}^{*}TN
, given by the identifications

p_{1}^{*}TN\cong p_{1}^{*}\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)\oplus\mathcal{C},\quad\left(p_{1}^{*}TN\right)_{C_{n}}=\ker T_{n}q\oplus C_{n}.

The subbundle
\mathcal{C}\subset p_{1}^{*}TN
is called the
Cartan distribution
. We recall that its relevance arises from the fact that it detects which sections
\tau
of
q_{1}\colon J^{1}q\to X
are holonomic, i.e., of the form
\tau=j^{1}\sigma
, with
\sigma:X\to N
a section of
q\colon N\to X
. In fact, one has:

•

A local section
\tau\colon X\to J^{1}q
is holonomic if and only if it is tangent to the Cartan distribution, i.e., if

\operatorname{im}{\mathrm{d}}_{x}(p_{1}\circ\tau)\subset\mathcal{C}_{\tau(x)}\mbox{ for all $x\in\operatorname{dom}\tau$}.

The notion of a relative connection formalizes the type of behavior found in the previous example and generalizes the notion of Ehresmann connection.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
M\xrightarrow{p}N\xrightarrow{q}X
be two submersions. A
connection on
q
relative to
p
is a vector bundle
C\subset p^{*}TN
complementary to
p^{*}\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)
:

p^{*}TN\cong C\oplus p^{*}\ker({\mathrm{d}}q).

Equivalently, it is a map
c\colon M\to J^{1}q
such that
p_{1}\circ c=p
, where
p_{1}\colon J^{1}q\to N
is the projection.

A relative connection
C
on
M\xrightarrow{p}N\xrightarrow{q}X
gives rise to an algebroid
(A,p,{\mathrm{D}})
relative to
p
. For the vector bundle, we take
A=q^{*}TX
and, under the identification
p^{*}A\xrightarrow{\sim}C
, the anchor map corresponds to the inclusion

\rho\colon p^{*}A\xrightarrow{\sim}C\subset p^{*}TN.

If
X_{1},X_{2}\in\Gamma(TX)
are vector fields on
X
, then the relative bracket is determined by

[q^{*}X_{1},q^{*}X_{2}]_{{\mathrm{D}}}=p^{*}q^{*}[X_{1},X_{2}],

and extended to any sections of
A
through the Leibniz rule using the anchor. The resulting relative algebroid is an example of a relative algebroid with injective anchor.

To identify the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
of this relative algebroid one proceeds as follows. Recall from Section
1.5
that there is a canonical map
q_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX}\to q^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{TX}
, which pulls back to a map

p^{*}q_{*}\colon p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX}\to p^{*}q^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{TX}

The derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
is a lift of the de Rham differential
{\mathrm{d}}
in the sense that one has
p^{*}q_{*}(D)=p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}
, where
{\mathrm{d}}
is interpreted as a section
{\mathrm{d}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{TX})
.

\textstyle{p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}q_{*}}
\textstyle{p^{*}q^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{TX}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}}

This is the defining feature of the relative derivation associated to a relative connection.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
M\xrightarrow{p}N\xrightarrow{q}X
be two submersions. There is a one-to-one correspondence between
p
-relative connections
C
on
q
and sections
D\in\Gamma(p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX})
with
p^{*}q_{*}{\mathrm{D}}=p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}
. In particular, given
C
the corresponding derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{C}
is determined by

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}_{C}f=(p^{*}{\mathrm{d}}f)|_{C},&\text{if }f\in C^{\infty}(N),\\
{\mathrm{D}}_{C}(q^{*}\alpha)=p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}\alpha,&\text{if }\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X).\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
{\mathrm{D}}\in\Gamma(p^{*}\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX})
. The equation

(p^{*}q_{*}{\mathrm{D}})(q^{*}f)=p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}(q^{*}f),\quad(f\in C^{\infty}(X)),

holds if and only if for any vector field
X\in\Gamma(TX)
one has

(p^{*}q_{*}{\mathrm{D}})(q^{*}f)(p^{*}q^{*}X)=(p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}(q^{*}f))(p^{*}q^{*}X)

and this is equivalent to:

\rho_{{\mathrm{D}}}(p^{*}q^{*}X)(p^{*}q^{*}f)=p^{*}q^{*}X(f).

This last equation holds if and only if
\rho_{{\mathrm{D}}}\colon p^{*}A\to p^{*}TN
is injective with image a subbundle
C
complementary to
p^{*}\ker({\mathrm{d}}q)
.

On the other hand, the equation

(p^{*}q_{*}{\mathrm{D}})(q^{*}\alpha)=p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}(q^{*}\alpha),\quad(\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(X)),

holds if and only if for any vector fields
X_{1},X_{2}\in\Gamma(TX)
one has

(p^{*}q_{*}{\mathrm{D}})(q^{*}\alpha)(p^{*}q^{*}X_{1},p^{*}q^{*}X_{2})=(p^{*}q^{*}{\mathrm{d}}(q^{*}\alpha))(p^{*}q^{*}X_{1},p^{*}q^{*}X_{2}),

and in terms of the bracket of
{\mathrm{D}}
this is equivalent to

[q^{*}X_{1},q^{*}X_{2}]_{{\mathrm{D}}}=p^{*}q^{*}[X_{1},X_{2}],

so the result follows.
∎

The Cartan distribution
\mathcal{C}\subset p_{1}^{*}TN
in Example
5.1
, being a relative distribution for the submersions
J^{1}q\xrightarrow{p_{1}}N\xrightarrow{q}X
, has an associated relative algebroid with derivation

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(q^{*}TX)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(q^{*}_{1}TX),

where
q_{1}:=q\circ p_{1}\colon J^{1}q\to X
. Notice that, by the previous proposition, one has

(5.1)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}f=(p_{1}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}f)|_{\mathcal{C}},&\text{if }f\in C^{\infty}(N),\\
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}(q^{*}\alpha)=q_{1}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}\alpha,&\text{if }\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X).\end{cases}

Definition 5.4
.

The derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}
is called the
Cartan derivation
.

The previous proposition leads to the following description of
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
q\colon N\to X
be a submersion. The bundle of first jets is naturally isomorphic to

J^{1}q\cong q_{*}^{-1}({\mathrm{d}}):=\left\{{\mathrm{D}}_{n}\in\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX}\ |\ q_{*}({\mathrm{D}}_{n})={\mathrm{d}}_{q(n)}\right\},

where the natural isomorphism is given by restriction of the symbol map
\sigma\colon\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX}\to\operatorname{Hom}(q^{*}TX,TN)
.
The relative algebroid
(q^{*}TX,p_{1},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}})
has classifying map the resulting inclusion

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 22.58913pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.58913pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{c_{{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}}\colon J^{1}q\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.58913pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.58913pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.58913pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proof.

The previous result applied to
p=\operatorname{id}
shows that for a fixed
n\in N
, we have

\left\{{\mathrm{D}}_{n}\in(\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q^{*}TX})_{n}\ |\ q_{*}({\mathrm{D}}_{n})={\mathrm{d}}_{q(n)}\right\}=\{C_{n}\subset T_{n}N:C_{n}\oplus\ker({\mathrm{d}}_{n}q)=T_{n}N\}.

The left side is canonically isomorphic to
(J^{1}q)_{n}
.
∎

Example 5.6

(Cartan derivation in local coordinates)
.

Let us assume that we have fixed local charts
(V,x^{i})
for
X
and
(U=q^{-1}(V),x^{i},u^{a})
for
N
, so that

q\colon N\to X,\quad q(x^{i},u^{a})=x^{i}.

Then we have an induced chart
(p_{1}^{-1}(U),x^{i},u^{a},u^{a}_{i})
on the total space of the first jet bundle so that

p_{1}\colon J^{1}q\to N,\quad p_{1}(x^{i},u^{a},u^{a}_{i})=(x^{i},u^{a}).

Also, let
e_{i}=\partial_{x^{i}}
be the corresponding local frame for
TX
with dual coframe
\theta^{i}={\mathrm{d}}x^{i}
. Then a form
\alpha\in\Omega^{k}(q^{*}TV)
can be expressed as

\alpha=\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\alpha_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}(x,u)\,{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{k}},

and it follows from (
5.1
) that the Cartan derivation acts on such a form as the total exterior derivative

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}\alpha=\sum_{j}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}(D_{j}\alpha_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}})\,{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{k}},

where

D_{j}f:=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{a}\frac{\partial f}{\partial u^{a}}u^{a}_{j}.

One obtains higher order Cartan derivations by considering the higher order jet spaces of the submersion
q\colon N\to X
. For any integer
k\geq 1
, one constructs a connection on
J^{k-1}q\to X
relative to
p_{k}\colon J^{k}q\to J^{k-1}q
by considering first the inclusion
J^{k}q\hookrightarrow J^{1}(J^{k-1}q)
and then restricting the Cartan distribution
\mathcal{C}\subset p_{k}^{*}TJ^{k-1}q\to J^{1}(J^{k-1}q)
to
J^{k}q
. The resulting relative connection will also be called the (higher order)
Cartan distribution
.

The higher-order Cartan distribution, being a relative distribution for the submersions
J^{k}q\xrightarrow{p_{k}}J^{k-1}q\xrightarrow{q_{k-1}}X
, has an associated relative algebroid with derivation

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(q^{*}_{k-1}TX)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(q^{*}_{k}TX).

We also call
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}
the (higher order)
Cartan derivation
. Again, applying Proposition
5.3
, one has

(5.2)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}f=(p_{k}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}f)|_{\mathcal{C}},&\text{if }f\in C^{\infty}(J^{k-1}q),\\
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}(q_{k-1}^{*}\alpha)=q_{k}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}\alpha,&\text{if }\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X).\end{cases}

Example 5.7

(Higher order Cartan derivation in local coordinates)
.

Similar to the case of first order, we can describe the higher order Cartan derivation in local coordinates as follows. We let
n=\dim X
(number of independent variables) and
d=\dim N-\dim X
(number of dependent variables) and we fix local charts
(V,x^{i})
for
X
and
(U=q_{k-1}^{-1}(V),x^{i},u^{a},u^{a}_{J})
for
J^{k-1}q
, with
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{r})
all unordered
r
-tuples of integers with
1\leq j_{l}\leq n
, and
\#J=r\leq k-1
. Then a form
\alpha\in\Omega^{k}(q_{k-1}^{*}TV)
can be expressed as

\alpha=\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\alpha_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}(x,u,u_{J})\,{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{k}},

and the Cartan derivation (
5.2
) acts on such a form as the total exterior derivative

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}\alpha=\sum_{j}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}(D_{j}\alpha_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}})\,{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{k}},

where now

D_{j}f:=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\sum_{\#J<k}\frac{\partial^{\#J}f}{\partial u_{J}^{a}}u^{a}_{j,J}.

5.2.
The relative algebroid of a PDE

We now wish to associate a relative algebroid to a PDE. By the latter we mean:

Definition 5.8
.

A
partial differential equation (PDE)
of order
k
on a submersion
q\colon N\to X
is a submanifold
E\subseteq J^{k}q
. A
solution
to
E
is a (local) section
\sigma
of
q\colon N\to X
such that
\operatorname{im}j^{k}\sigma\subset E
.

If we assume that the image
p_{k}(E)\subseteq J^{k-1}q
is a manifold and that the map
q_{k}=q_{k-1}\circ p_{k}\colon E\to X
is a submersion, then the Cartan distribution restricts to a connection of
q_{k-1}\colon p_{k}(E)\to X
relative to the submersions
E\xrightarrow{p_{k}}p_{k}(E)\xrightarrow{q_{k-1}}X
. The corresponding relative algebroid of the PDE is an algebroid
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
relative to the submersion
p_{k}\colon E\to p_{k}(E)
, and the derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{E}
is determined by

(5.3)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}_{E}f=(p_{k}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}f)|_{\mathcal{C}},&\text{if }f\in C^{\infty}(p_{k}(E)),\\
{\mathrm{D}}_{E}(q_{k-1}^{*}\alpha)=i^{*}_{E}q_{k}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}\alpha,&\text{if }\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X),\end{cases}

where
i_{E}\colon E\hookrightarrow J^{k}q
is the inclusion.

Definition 5.9
.

We call
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
the
relative algebroid of the PDE

E\subset J^{k}q
.

Remark 5.10
.

If we make the weaker assumption that the PDE
E\subseteq J^{k}q
intersects the fibers of
p_{k}\colon J^{k}q\to J^{k-1}q
in submanifolds of a fixed dimension, then, by Proposition
4.12
, the relative algebroid
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}})
associated to the Cartan distribution can be restricted to
E
. Hence, we still have a relative algebroid
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,\overline{\nabla},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
associated to
E
. The results that follow are valid in this more general setting, replacing
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
by this algebroid relative to a foliation. To simplify the exposition, we choose to stay within the framework of algebroids relative to a submersion.

Our next result shows that the relative algebroid of the PDE encodes its solutions.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
E\subset J^{k}q
be a PDE with relative algebroid
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
. Then germs of solutions to
E
are in 1-1 correspondence with germs of realizations of
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
modulo diffeomorphisms.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\colon U\to N
be a local solution of
E
. Then we construct a realization
(\theta,r)\colon TP\to q_{k}^{*}TX
of
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
by setting:

P:=U,\quad r(x):=j^{k}_{x}\sigma,\quad\theta(v_{x})=(r(x),v_{x}).

That
\theta
preserves anchors is clear. Using (
5.3
) one finds that for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(X)

\theta^{*}{\mathrm{D}}_{E}(q^{*}_{k-1}\alpha)=\theta^{*}i^{*}_{E}q_{k}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}\alpha={\mathrm{d}}\alpha={\mathrm{d}}\theta^{*}\alpha.

Conversely, assume that
(P,r,\theta)
is a realization around
p\in P
of
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
such that
r(p)=e\in E
. Then the map
q_{k}\circ r
is a local diffeomorphism, so in a neighborhood of
p\in P
it factors through a (local) section
\tau\colon X\to E
.

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{k}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}

The compatibility of
(r,\theta)
with the anchor, gives

\operatorname{im}{\mathrm{d}}_{x}(p_{k}\circ\tau)\subset\mathcal{C}_{\tau(x)}\mbox{ for all $x\in\operatorname{dom}\tau$}.

So
\tau
is tangent to the Cartan distribution, and we can conclude that it is holonomic. Hence,
\tau=j^{k}\sigma
for a local section
\sigma\colon X\to N
, which is the desired local solution of
E
.

∎

5.3.
Prolongation and integrability of PDEs

We will now show that the formal theory of prolongations
[
20
]
for PDEs coincides with the prolongation theory for the associated relative algebroids.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
E\subset J^{k}q
be a PDE with relative algebroid
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
. Then:

(i)

E
is a 1-integrable PDE if and only if the relative algebroid
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
is 1-integrable;

(ii)

If
E
is a 1-integrable PDE, then the relative algebroid corresponding to the prolongation
E^{(1)}\subset J^{k+1}q
is the prolongation of
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{E})
.

In particular, a PDE is formally integrable if and only if its associated relative algebroid is.

Remark 5.13

(Variational bicomplex)
.

When
E=J^{1}\pi
Theorem
5.12
shows that the prolongations of
E
correspond to the higher order Cartan derivations. From their expression in local coordinates (see Example
5.7
), one sees that they assemble together into the horizontal differential of the first row of the variational bicomplex (see, e.g.,
[
5
]
). In other words, the profinite Lie algebroid corresponding to the prolongation tower has as derivation the horizontal differential of the variational bicomplex:

\textstyle{{\left(\pi_{\infty}^{*}TX,{\mathrm{d}}_{H}\right)\colon\ldots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{k}^{*}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{k-1}^{*}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{*}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{*}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{J^{\infty}\pi\colon\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J^{k}\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{k}}
\textstyle{J^{k-1}\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J^{1}\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{N.}

Proof of Theorem
5.12
.

Let us consider first the case
E=J^{k}q
, so
{\mathrm{D}}_{E}={\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}
. On the one hand,
E
as a PDE, has first prolongation

E^{(1)}=J^{k+1}q\cong\left\{j^{1}_{y}\tau\colon\tau\in\Gamma(J^{k}q)\text{ holonomic}\right\}\subset J^{1}(J^{k}q).

On the other hand, by Corollary
5.5
, we have the following description of the first jet bundle of
J^{k}q
:

J^{1}(J^{k}q)=\left\{{\mathrm{D}}_{y}\in\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q_{k}^{*}TX}\colon(q_{k})_{*}{\mathrm{D}}_{y}={\mathrm{d}}_{q_{k}(y)}\right\}.

This gives a description of
E^{(1)}
in terms of derivations as

\displaystyle E^{(1)}\cong

\displaystyle\left\{{\mathrm{D}}_{y}\in\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q_{k}^{*}TX}\colon(q_{k})_{*}{\mathrm{D}}_{y}={\mathrm{d}}_{q_{k}(y)},\ {\mathrm{D}}_{y}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\mathrm{D}}_{y}\in\mathcal{D}^{1}_{q_{k}^{*}TX}\colon(q_{k})_{*}{\mathrm{D}}_{y}=({\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}})_{p_{k}(y)},\ {\mathrm{D}}_{y}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}}=0\right\}

which is precisely the first prolongation space of the relative algebroid
(q_{k-1}^{*}TX,p_{k},{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathcal{C}})
. It follows that the theorem holds in this case.

For general
E\subset J^{k}q
, the result follows because the first prolongation of
E
can be described as
E^{(1)}=J^{1}E\cap J^{k+1}q
in
J^{1}(J^{k}q)
, which corresponds to the first prolongation of the relative algebroid using the description for
J^{k+1}q
in terms of derivations. This proves both (i) and (ii).
∎

Example 5.14
.

We illustrate the theorem with the simple PDE
u_{x}=y
, where
u=u(x,y)
. As a manifold, this PDE has coordinates
(u_{y},u,x,y)
and sits inside

\{(y,u_{y},u,x,y)\}\subset J^{1}\pi=\{(u_{x},u_{y},u,x,y)\},

where
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the projection
\pi(u,x,y)=(x,y)
.
The corresponding relative algebroid is obtained by restricting the Cartan derivation to
E
, so it can be described by the trivial vector bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with derivation
{\mathrm{D}}
determined by

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}\theta^{1}={\mathrm{D}}\theta^{2}=0,\\
{\mathrm{D}}x=\theta^{1},\\
{\mathrm{D}}y=\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{D}}u=y\theta^{1}+u_{y}\theta^{2}.\end{cases}

The free variable is
u_{y}
, so to compute the prolongation we start with an extension of
{\mathrm{D}}
, which is determined by
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}u_{y}=u_{xy}\theta^{1}+u_{yy}\theta^{2}
. We find that
\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}
must satisfy

0=\tilde{{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathrm{D}}u=\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{1}+(u_{xy}\theta^{1}+u_{yy}\theta^{2})\wedge\theta^{2}=(u_{xy}-1)\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}.

We find that the 1st prolongation of the relative algebroid is characterized by
u_{xy}=1
and that
u_{yy}
is the new variable. This corresponds exactly to the relative algebroid underlying the prolongation
E^{(1)}\subset J^{2}\pi
of the PDE: the latter is given by the equations
\{u_{x}=y,u_{xx}=0,u_{xy}=1\}
, so
E^{(1)}
is parametrized by
\{(u_{yy},u_{y},u,x,y)\}
.

Remark 5.15

(PDEs with symmetries and Pfaffian fibrations)
.

Symmetries of PDEs are described by pseudogroups of diffeomorphisms (see, e.g.,
[
26
]
). One may wonder how symmetries and reduction of PDEs can be dealt with in the context of relative algebroids. In
[
1
,
2
,
10
,
11
,
30
]
, a framework for studying PDEs with symmetries was developed using so-called Pfaffian fibrations and Pfaffian actions of Pfaffian groupoids. In
[
32
]
, we show that relative algebroids underlie Pfaffian fibrations and that Pfaffian actions induce symmetries of the associated relative algebroids.

6.
Postlude: an example

In this final section, we will revisit Example
0.2
from the Introduction and we discuss it using the framework developed in the paper. This example, considered by Bryant in
[
8
, §5.1]
, is simple enough that it can be solved directly, but it is extremely insightful to study it from the perspective of relative derivations.

As discussed in the Introduction, the existence and classification problem of surfaces with a metric whose Gauss curvature satisfies
|\nabla K|=1
is governed by the equations

(6.1)

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{1}=-\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}=\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{1},\\
{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{3}=K\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2},\\
{\mathrm{d}}K=\cos(\varphi)\theta^{1}+\sin(\varphi)\theta^{2}.\end{cases}

These equations define a derivation
{\mathrm{D}}_{A}
on the trivial vector bundle
A:=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\to\mathbb{R}
, relative to the projection
p\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{R}
has coordinate
K
and
\mathbb{S}^{1}
has coordinate
\varphi
. Here
\{\theta^{1},\theta^{2},\theta^{3}\}
is a basis of sections of
A^{*}
and if we let
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
be the dual basis of sections of
A
, the anchor of this relative algebroid is given by

\displaystyle\rho(e_{1})

\displaystyle=\cos(\varphi)\partial_{K},

\displaystyle\rho(e_{2})

\displaystyle=\sin(\varphi)\partial_{K},

\displaystyle\rho(e_{3})

\displaystyle=0,

while the bracket takes the form

\displaystyle[e_{1},e_{2}]

\displaystyle=-Ke_{3},

\displaystyle[e_{2},e_{3}]

\displaystyle=-e_{1},

\displaystyle[e_{3},e_{1}]

\displaystyle=-e_{2}.

Using this expression for the anchor, a straightforward computation shows that this relative algebroid has tableau map
\tau\colon\ker({\mathrm{d}}p)\to p^{*}\operatorname{Hom}(\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}},T\mathbb{R})
given by

\tau(\partial_{\varphi})=\left(-\sin(\varphi)\theta^{1}+\cos(\varphi)\theta^{2}\right)\otimes\partial_{K}.

In the sequel, it will be convenient to use the following notation for the expression appearing in the tableau:

\partial_{\varphi}({\mathrm{d}}K):=-\sin(\varphi)\theta^{1}+\cos(\varphi)\theta^{2}.

All higher prolongations can be explicitly computed, giving the full prolongation tower

\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{k}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R},}

where
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
has coordinates
(c_{k-1},\ldots,c_{0},\varphi,K)
, and the relative derivation is determined by (
6.1
) together with

(6.2)

{\mathrm{D}}c_{k}=P_{k}[c_{0},\dots,c_{k}]{\mathrm{d}}K+c_{k+1}\partial_{\varphi}({\mathrm{d}}K)

where
P_{k}[c_{0},\dots,c_{k}]
are polynomials given by

\displaystyle P_{0}[c_{0}]

\displaystyle=-c_{0}^{2}-K

\displaystyle P_{2m-1}[c_{0},\dots,c_{2m-1}]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\binom{2m+1}{i+1}c_{i}c_{2m-1-i},

\displaystyle P_{2m}[c_{0},\dots,c_{2m}]

\displaystyle=-\binom{2m+1}{m}c_{m}^{2}-\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\binom{2m+2}{i+1}c_{i}c_{2m-i},

for
m\geq 1
. Note that we can also interpret (
6.2
) as defining a profinite derivation on
\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}

Equation (
6.2
) suggests using the new global coframe
\{{\mathrm{d}}K,\partial_{\varphi}({\mathrm{d}}K),\theta^{3}\}
, for which the anchor is decoupled such that the profinite part is concentrated in one basis vector only. The frame
\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\}
dual to this coframe is given by

(6.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{1}&=(\cos\varphi)e_{1}+(\sin\varphi)e_{2}\\
b_{2}&=-(\sin\varphi)e_{1}+(\cos\varphi)e_{2}-c_{0}e_{3}\\
b_{3}&=e_{3}.\end{split}

The profinite Lie algebroid
\underline{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
, with respect to the new frame, has bracket

(6.4)

[b_{1},b_{2}]=-c_{0}b_{2},\qquad[b_{1},b_{3}]=[b_{2},b_{3}]=0.

and anchor

(6.5)

\displaystyle\rho_{\infty}(b_{1})

\displaystyle=\partial_{K}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}P_{k}[c_{0},\dots,c_{k}]\partial_{c_{k}},

\displaystyle\rho_{\infty}(b_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k+1}\partial_{c_{k}},

\displaystyle\rho_{\infty}(b_{3})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\varphi}.

The algebroid decouples as the product of
T\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
\underline{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}
, where the latter has global frame
\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
. It will be convenient to set
\Sigma^{k}:=\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}
, and denote by

\mathcal{A}:=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\to\Sigma^{\infty}:=\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}

the algebroid with global frame
\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
.

The vector field
\rho_{\infty}(b_{1})
is levelwise profinite in nature, and its flow can be explicitly computed in terms of the solution of a Riccati equation. However, by
[
13
, Lemma 3.3]
, the vector field
\rho_{\infty}(b_{2})
has no flow defined on profinite open subsets. For this reason, there cannot be a smooth groupoid, whose source fibers are 2-dimensional manifolds; if there was one, the right-invariant vector field corresponding to
b_{2}
would have a flow restricted to each source fiber, which would descend to a flow of
\rho_{\infty}(b_{2})
on the base
\Sigma^{\infty}
.

The vector field
\rho_{\infty}(b_{2})
does have a flow on resolutions of the sequence
\Sigma^{k+1}\to\Sigma^{k}
other than the profinite limit
\Sigma^{\infty}
. For this, note that this vector field does not depend on the coordinate
K
on
\Sigma^{\infty}
, and lives entirely on
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, with coordinates
c_{\infty}=(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. So let
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
C^{\omega}(\mathbb{R})
be the spaces of smooth, respectively analytic, functions on
\mathbb{R}
. Also, we denote by
C^{\omega}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
and
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
the corresponding spaces of germs around 0. The jet map
j^{\infty}_{0}
relates the vector field
\partial_{t}
to the vector field
\rho_{\infty}(b_{2})
, viewed as derivations of these spaces:

\textstyle{(C^{\omega}_{0}(\mathbb{R}),\partial_{t})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\infty}_{0}}
\textstyle{(C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}),\partial_{t})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\infty}_{0}}
\textstyle{(C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}),\partial_{t})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\infty}_{0}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\sum c_{k+1}\partial_{c_{k}})}

In other words, these spaces all provide different resolutions with topologies vastly different from the profinite topology on
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
. Also, observe that

•

On the space of analytic germs
C^{\omega}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, the vector field
\partial_{t}
has a flow
\Phi_{t}
, given by

\Phi_{t}(\germ_{0}(f(\cdot)))=\germ_{0}(f(\cdot+t))

whenever
t
is in the maximal domain to which the germ of
f
can be extended;

•

On
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, the vector field
\partial_{t}
has no well-defined flow, since there are many distinct integral curves through a point, due to the existence of flat functions;

•

On
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the vector field
\partial_{t}
is complete with smooth flow
\Phi_{t}(f)=f(\cdot+t)
.

•

On
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, as we already mentioned, the vector field
\sum_{k}c_{k+1}\partial_{c_{k}}
has no flow.

It is natural to pull back the algebroid
\mathcal{A}\to\Sigma^{\infty}
to the resolutions on which
\partial_{t}
has a flow.

Let us focus now on the resolution by analytic germs. The image of the infinite jet map
j^{\infty}_{0}\colon C^{\omega}_{0}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
can be identified with the space of convergent power series:

\mathbb{R}^{\omega}:=\left\{c_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\colon\limsup_{k}\left(\frac{|c_{k}|}{k!}\right)^{1/k}<\infty\right\},

and it seems natural to consider on
\mathbb{R}^{\omega}
the smooth structure of
C^{\omega}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
rather than the profinite smooth structure on
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
. In a similar vein, we let

\Sigma^{\omega}:=\mathbb{R}^{\omega}\times\mathbb{R}\subset\Sigma^{\infty}.

It turns out that
\rho_{\infty}(b_{1})
is also tangent to
\Sigma^{\omega}
. This is because when
c_{\infty}=(c_{k})_{k}
is a convergent power series, then the sequence
P_{\infty}(c_{\infty}):=\left(P_{k}(c_{0},\dots,c_{k})\right)_{k}
also defines a convergent power series (with the same radius of convergence!). Therefore, the algebroid
\mathcal{A}\to\Sigma^{\infty}
can be restricted to

\mathcal{A}^{\omega}\colon\mathcal{A}\big|_{\Sigma^{\omega}}\to\Sigma^{\omega}.

Due to the existence of flows of both
\rho_{\infty}(b_{1})
and
\rho_{\infty}(b_{2})
, the algebroid partitions
\Sigma^{\omega}
into well-defined (finite-dimensional) leaves.

In this way, the original classification problem can be solved on this space: it is governed by the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-structure algebroid
T\mathbb{S}^{1}\times A^{\omega}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\Sigma^{\omega}
whose canonical
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-integration is the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-structure groupoid
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})\times\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\Sigma^{\omega}
. The source fibers of this groupoid, which are of the form
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times s_{\mathcal{G}^{\omega}}^{-1}(c_{\infty})
, are the coframe bundles of maximal
analytic
simply connected solutions to the realization problem!

The appropriate smooth structure on the total space of this groupoid has yet to be studied, but we believe it is a type of diffeological groupoid that differentiates to the given algebroid, in the sense of Aintablian and Blohmann
[
4
]
.

6.1.
Solutions with additional symmetry

Even without delving into the complicated theory of infinite-dimensional geometry, a glance at the profinite algebroid
T\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\Sigma^{\infty}
can already lead to interesting insights and solutions. For example, it is possible to find solutions by looking at the locus where the anchor drops rank. This amounts to imposing extra symmetry on realizations.

The expression for the anchor shows that the algebroid
\mathcal{A}\to\Sigma^{\infty}
drops rank on the subspace

\Sigma^{\infty}_{0}=\{(c_{\infty},K)\in\Sigma^{\infty}:c_{k}=0\mbox{ for $k\geq 1$}\}.

This locus is
finite-dimensional
and one obtains a restricted algebroid with anchor and bracket given by

\rho(b_{1})=\partial_{K}-(K+c_{0}^{2})\partial_{c_{0}},\qquad\rho(b_{2})=0,\qquad[b_{1},b_{2}]=-c_{0}b_{2}.

Geometrically, we are imposing an extra symmetry, namely translation in the direction orthogonal to
\nabla K
.

The restricted algebroid
\mathcal{A}\big|_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{0}}
is integrable and its source-simply connected integration, by the work of Fernandes and Struchiner
[
19
]
, represents the stack of complete, simply connected, solutions of the realization problem with translational symmetry. The integrating groupoid can be described in terms of solutions to a Riccati equation: for
(c_{0},K_{0})\in\Sigma^{\infty}_{0}
, let
R_{(c_{0},K_{0})}
be the unique solution to the Riccati equation

\begin{cases}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}R}{{\mathrm{d}}K}=-K-R^{2},\\
R(K_{0})=c_{0},\end{cases}

which is defined on an open interval
I_{(c_{0},K_{0})}
. The space of arrows is the manifold

\mathcal{G}\big|_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{0}}=\left\{(t_{1},t_{2},c_{0},K)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ K+t_{1}\in I_{(c_{0},K)}\right\}.

The source and target are

\sigma(t_{1},t_{2},c_{0},K)=(c_{0},K),\qquad\tau(t_{1},t_{2},c_{0},K)=(R_{(c_{0},K)}(K+t_{1}),K+t_{1}),

and multiplication is given by

\displaystyle\left(t_{1},t_{2},K+t_{1},R_{(c_{0},K)}(K+t_{1})\right)\cdot(s_{1},s_{2},c_{0},K)

\displaystyle=\left(t_{1}+s_{1},\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s_{1}}R_{(c_{0},K)}(K+s){\mathrm{d}}s\right)t_{2}+s_{2},c_{0},K\right).

The groupoid
\mathcal{G}\big|_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{0}}
has isotropy
\mathbb{R}
and carries a groupoid involution

(t_{1},t_{2},c_{0},K)\mapsto(t_{1},-t_{2},c_{0},K),

so the isometry groups of the solutions corresponding to the source fibers are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ltimes\mathbb{R}
.

6.2.
Other solutions

In general, any finite-rank profinite Lie algebroid can be pulled back to a sufficiently regular integral manifold yielding a finite-dimensional Lie algebroid, which can then be integrated to obtain realizations of the original profinite Lie algebroid. Such integral manifolds can be constructed using flows.

To apply this principle to our example, let us describe explicitly the flow
\Phi^{t}(c_{\infty},K)
of the vector field
\rho_{\infty}(b_{1})
. Due to the specific shape of this vector field, the flow has components

\Phi^{t}(c_{\infty},K)=(K+t,(\Phi^{t}_{k}(c_{k},\dots,c_{0},K))_{k}).

As before, the zeroth component of the flow of
\rho_{\infty}(b_{1})
is given by

\Phi^{t}_{0}(c_{0},K)=R_{(c_{0},K)}(K+t),\quad t+K\in I_{(c_{0},K)}.

All other components are also defined on the same time interval. This is because for
k\geq 1
the integral curves obey a first order ODE of the form

\dot{c}_{k}=-(k+2)c_{k}c_{0}+F_{k}(c_{1}(t),\dots c_{k-1}(t)),

which has a solution wherever it is defined.

Integral manifolds of
\mathcal{A}^{\omega}
can be constructed from this flow as follows. Given
c_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}^{\omega}
, let
c_{\infty}(t_{2})
be the maximal integral curve of
\rho_{\infty}(b_{2})
, i.e.
c_{\infty}(t_{2})=j^{\infty}_{t_{2}}f
for some maximally extended analytic function
f
. Then we obtain (in general, non-injective) parameterizations of integral curves

\{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\colon t_{2}\in I_{f},K_{0}+t_{1}\in I_{(c_{0}(t_{2}),K_{0})}\}\ni(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto\Phi^{t_{1}}(c_{\infty}(t_{2}),K_{0})\in\Sigma^{\omega}.

In particular, we obtain integral manifolds of
\mathcal{A}^{\omega}
which are diffeomorphic to:

•

an interval, when
c_{\infty}\in\Sigma^{\infty}_{0}
;

•

a cylinder, when
c_{\infty}
is the jet of a periodic analytic function;

•

a contractible open set in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in all other cases.

Although we currently do not have the techniques to compute the entire leaves of the algebroid
\mathcal{A}^{\omega}
, the algebroid
can
be pulled back to each of these integral manifolds. The integration of the resulting algebroid (which is just the homotopy groupoids of the integral manifolds) gives solutions to the realization problem. The isometry groups of solutions obtained this way come entirely from the symmetry of the function
f
associated to the jet
c_{\infty}
: it can be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ltimes\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
or 1, depending on whether
f
is periodic and/or has reflectional symmetry.

7.
Outlook

In this paper, we have established the foundational framework of the theory of relative algebroids. There are numerous directions to further explore. Below, we outline a few of those and their relationships with existing literature.

7.1.
PDEs with symmetries

In the context of the formal theory of PDEs, one of the key advantages of the framework of relative algebroids is its stability under quotients by symmetries. In Section
5
, we saw that every PDE has an associated canonical relative algebroid whose realizations, up to diffeomorphism, correspond to solutions of the PDE. As shown in
[
32
]
, symmetries of PDEs lead to symmetries of the underlying relative algebroid. Classification problems in geometry are often governed by PDEs with large symmetry groups. Our ultimate goal is to provide a rigorous explanation of how Bryant’s equations arise from a geometric problem formulated as a PDE with symmetries.

We expect that the quotient algebroid associated with a PDE with symmetries is related to various existing approaches to symmetries in the literature. For example, the space of differential invariants of a PDE with (pseudogroup) symmetries (see, e.g.,
[
24
,
28
]
) should be related to the exterior algebra of the quotient relative algebroid.

Another connection to the existing literature arises through Pfaffian fibrations and Pfaffian actions
[
1
,
2
,
10
,
11
,
30
]
, which has been established in
[
32
]
.

7.2.
Relative
G
-structure algebroids

The Bryant-Cartan existence result for realizations in the analytic setting (Theorem
3.20
) provides local manifolds with coframes that solve the realization problem. However, in many realization problems, such as those arising from Riemannian manifolds or more general
G
-structures, the realization problem is naturally formulated in terms of coframes on the orthonormal frame bundle or a principal
G
-bundle. In general, the local solutions obtained from the Bryant-Cartan theorem do not yield such principal bundles, as the presence of a structure group is not accounted for. Therefore, it is desirable to incorporate structure groups into the theory of relative algebroids.

In the case of finite-dimensional Lie algebroids, a theory of
G
-structure algebroids and
G
-structure groupoids has been developed in
[
19
]
. One important open question is to find the right framework that incorporates the structure group in realization problems defined by relative algebroids as well.

7.3.
Profinite Lie algebroids

Profinite-dimensional manifolds and bundles appear extensively in the theory of formal PDEs (see, e.g.,
[
1
,
3
,
22
,
27
]
). Hence, their emergence in the theory of relative algebroids is not surprising: the base space of the prolongation tower of a relative algebroid is the space of formal realizations modulo symmetries.

There are several challenges regarding the existence of smooth groupoids integrating the prolongation tower of a relative algebroid. In fact, we suspect that such groupoids may not exist for any prolongation tower. Intuitively, this stems from the fact that there is no “continuous” way to assign a smooth function to each jet. Nevertheless, there are two interesting alternatives.

•

For real analytic relative Lie algebroids, it is possible to restrict to the space of “convergent power series”. Over this subspace, the profinite algebroid has well-defined leaves and can be leafwise integrated into a groupoid. Understanding the global realizations requires a combination of finite-dimensional Lie integration theory and tools from analytic continuation. The smooth structure of the integrating groupoid determines the moduli stack of global real-analytic realizations. We don’t understand the nature of this object yet, but recent work of Aintablian and Blohmann
[
4
]
could shed some light.

•

Another possible approach, suggested to us by Ivan Contreras, is to consider
formal Lie groupoids
(see, e.g.,
[
12
,
14
]
) integrating a profinite Lie groupoid. Since formal Lie groupoids arise from power series, it seems plausible that integrating objects of such nature for profinite Lie algebroids may exist.

7.4.
Control theory

Example
3.15
illustrates how a control system arises from a relative algebroid defined by a relative vector field. The interaction between control theory and relative algebroids should also work in the other direction.

As a particular example, the notion of controllability (the equivalence relation of points connected by realizations – see
[
23
]
) should also be present for relative algebroids. This notion should induce a partition of the base of a relative algebroid into invariant submanifolds. This bidirectional interaction suggests deeper connections between the two fields.
